# RESEARCH — Chicken chains that got too greedy (Canada), assembled 28 Sep 2026

FILE: note_fc_structure.md

# Structure note: "7 Fried Chicken Chains in Canada That Got Too Greedy and Paid the Price"

Prepared 2026-09-28 from seven transcripts. All word counts below are measured by script (markers at each beat boundary, `[music]` and `>>` stripped), not estimated. Channel prose runs 6.5 characters per word, so the 21,000–23,000 character target is 3,240–3,550 words; aim for 3,350–3,400.

Source files (all in the scratchpad):
- tr_CC_greedy_restaurants_53K.txt — channel, 16 Feb 2026, 2,918 w / 18,901 ch. The construction being redone.
- tr_CC_friedchicken_105K.txt — channel, 13 Jan 2026, 3,334 w / 21,630 ch.
- tr_CC_friedchicken_72K.txt — channel, 21 May 2026, 3,439 w / 22,515 ch.
- tr_BC_coffee_caught_162K.txt — Broken Canada, 21 Sep 2026, 4,577 w / 27,572 ch.
- tr_BA_doritos_980K.txt — Bagholder Association, 2,338 w / 14,940 ch.
- tr_BA_pepsi_910K.txt — Bagholder Association, 2,560 w / 15,298 ch.
- tr_BA_krispykreme_464K.txt — Bagholder Association, 2,547 w / 16,219 ch.

---

## 1. Measured skeletons

### 1a. Channel, "7 Canadian Restaurants That Got Too Greedy and Paid the Price" (Feb 2026, 52.6K, 5.8x median)

Cold open, 108 words (3.7%). Five moves: (1) universal scene, "Every single day, millions of Canadians walk into a restaurant expecting a fair deal"; (2) the reversal, "But, some of the biggest restaurant chains in Canada decided that was not enough. They wanted more."; (3) the three sins as a triplet, "They raised prices. They shrank portions. They cut corners on quality and they thought you would not notice."; (4) the consequence promise with two concrete categories, "From plummeting brand trust scores to mass store closures"; (5) the frame line, "Chains that went from Canadian icons to cautionary tales." No subscribe ask in the open. No stated basis for the order.

Count and order logic: 7 to 1, announced as a countdown ("Number seven, Tim Hortons") but the order is rhetorical, not measured. #1 is "the restaurant that exemplifies how Canadian dining chains have lost touch," which is a judgment, not a metric. Five of seven beats (Tim Hortons, McDonald's, Starbucks, Subway, Wendy's) are US-owned QSR argued largely with US data; the two Canadian-owned casual chains (Swiss Chalet, Boston Pizza) occupy #2 and #1. The audience can and did ask why Boston Pizza, with 370-plus locations and net-zero unit growth, outranks Subway, which lost 7,600 units.

Beats (words, share, start position):
- #7 Tim Hortons 414 w (14.2%), starts 3.7%
- #6 McDonald's 320 w (11.0%), starts 17.9%
- #5 Starbucks 326 w (11.2%), starts 28.9%
- #4 Subway 353 w (12.1%), starts 40.0%
- #3 Wendy's 321 w (11.0%), starts 52.1%
- LIKE/SUBSCRIBE ASK 59 w (2.0%), starts 63.1%
- #2 Swiss Chalet 352 w (12.1%), starts 65.1%
- #1 Boston Pizza 402 w (13.8%), starts 77.2%
- Close A, "How to protect yourself" 191 w (6.5%), starts 91.0%
- Close B, moral + comment ask + sign-off 72 w (2.5%), starts 97.5%

Per-beat anatomy, measured inside the two longest beats:

Tim Hortons (414 w): recognition and history 73 w ("Founded in 1964 by hockey legend Tim Horton... The phrase double-double became part of the Canadian vocabulary"); ownership turn 46 w ("Then, everything changed. In 2014, Restaurant Brands International, backed by the global investment firm 3G Capital, purchased Tim Hortons for approximately 12 and 1/2 billion Canadian dollars"); the mechanism 67 w (cost cuts, franchisee association 2017, lawsuits); product complaints 42 w; consequence with sources 94 w (Leger, Gustavson, Maclean's); concession and second event 52 w ("Although the decisions were made at the franchise level, many customers blamed the corporate leadership"); latest dated event and closer 40 w ("In October 2025, Tim Hortons raised coffee prices...").

Boston Pizza (402 w): recognition 61 w; company's own explanation quoted 64 w ("According to the company's own financial disclosures, Boston Pizza restaurants were challenged in 2024 and early 2025 by..."); the numbers 70 w ("In 2023, Boston Pizza closed six restaurants while opening only one... franchise sales from closed restaurants in 2024 were $7.4 million compared to just $3.4 million"); review quotes and unsourced prices 79 w (the weak block); second disclosure quote on tariffs 60 w; closer 68 w.

Starbucks (326 w): recognition 63 w; aggregated-source claims 98 w ("according to multiple financial reports and news sources"); dated event 59 w ("In September 2025, Starbucks announced..."); company statement plus human detail 80 w ("The company said it was identifying stores that could not create the physical environment customers expect"); closer 26 w ("The company that once seemed untouchable is now fighting for survival").

Like ask (59 w, at 63.1%): "Now, if you are watching this and getting value from what we are sharing, take a second to subscribe and drop a comment below. Tell us which of these restaurants surprised you the most. Your engagement helps us reach more people who deserve to know the truth. Now, let us continue with the final two restaurants on our list." Sits after #3, before #2, and doubles as the comment ask.

The turn: none in the conventional sense (no "great" list). The pivot is "How to protect yourself." at 91%.

Close (263 w total): "How to protect yourself" is four numbered actions, each two to four sentences: First, vote with your wallet. Second, pay attention to portions ("Take photos of your meals. Compare them over time."). Third, support restaurants that offer genuine value. Fourth, cook more at home (with a Statistics Canada cite). Then the moral, "These seven chains had something in common. They all forgot a simple truth. The customer always has a choice." Then share ask, comment ask ("leave a comment telling us which restaurant on this list shocked you the most"), and the tagline "the truth is not always on the menu." No disclaimer. No alternatives named.

### 1b. Channel, "5 Chicken Fried Chains Canadians Must Never Eat From (3 That Are Great)" (Jan 2026, 105K)

Cold open 121 w (3.6%): second-person scene ("You're driving home after a long day, stomach growling"), the promise ("I'm exposing five chicken chains that are basically serving you disappointment in a bucket while charging premium prices. And then, I'll tell you about three chains that actually deliver"), and a subscribe ask inside the open ("Hit subscribe right now because once you hear what's really going on behind those fryers...").

Count and order: 5 avoid then 3 great, no numbering, no stated basis; order is "First up... Next... Moving on... Finally."

Beats: KFC 440 w (13.2%); Mary Brown's 415 w (12.4%); St-Hubert 368 w (11.0%); Church's 319 w (9.6%); Dixie Lee 357 w (10.7%); SUBSCRIBE ASK 89 w (2.7%) starting at 60.6%; Popeyes 340 w (10.2%); Jollibee 338 w (10.1%); Swiss Chalet 310 w (9.3%); close 237 w (7.1%) starting at 92.9%.

Per-beat anatomy, KFC (440 w): recognition 55 w (650 locations, Saskatoon 1953); thesis 22 w; review-site statistics 65 w ("1.6 star rating... Only 7% of customer issues get resolved"); review anecdotes 169 w (the bulk of the beat); prices 46 w; hygiene 60 w; closer 23 w ("KFC Canada was once the gold standard for fried chicken. Now it's a cautionary tale"). The whole avoid section is built on aggregated review-site material and food-safety anecdotes; there is no filing, no dated corporate event, no company statement in any of the five avoid beats except the St-Hubert acquisition line.

The turn (89 w at 60.6%): "Now, before I reveal the three chicken chains that actually deserve your hard-earned money, I want to ask you something... Hit that subscribe button and turn on notifications... Now, let's get into the chains that are actually doing it right."

Close (237 w): advice paragraph ("Look for chains that emphasize fresh preparation and transparent sourcing. Avoid places that can't tell you where their chicken comes from"), a local-independents recommendation, the binary ("You can keep supporting chains that serve you processed disappointment... or you can demand better"), share ask, subscribe ask, sign-off "choose your chicken wisely. Your stomach will thank you." No comment ask. No disclaimer.

### 1c. Channel, "I Investigated 7 Canadian Chicken Fried Chains (2 That Are Great)" (May 2026, 72K)

Cold open 270 w (7.9%), more than double the channel's other opens, spent entirely on EU dye regulation, the 2007 Southampton study, titanium dioxide and Health Canada before any chain is named. The promise: "Seven chains operating across this country are doing something dramatically different with that recipe. And only two of them are still worth your money." No subscribe ask in the open.

Count and order: 7 avoid, unnumbered, "worst offender" saved for last ("Now, let us get to the absolute worst offender on this avoid list"), then 2 great with the strongest last. Basis for order: implied severity, never stated.

Beats: KFC 398 w (11.6%); Popeyes 350 w (10.2%); Chick-fil-A 286 w (8.3%); Jollibee 298 w (8.7%); Dixie Lee 301 w (8.8%); Cluck Clucks 230 w (6.7%); SUBSCRIBE ASK 67 w (1.9%) starting at 62.0%; Chester's 342 w (9.9%); TURN/BRIDGE 146 w (4.2%) starting at 73.9%; Church's 248 w (7.2%); Mary Brown's 360 w (10.5%); close 143 w (4.2%) starting at 95.8%.

Per-beat anatomy, KFC: recognition 80 w; ownership 45 w ("The brand is now owned by Yum Brands... The Colonel on the bucket is American. The corporate decisions about your Canadian chicken are made in Louisville."); dated event 110 w (halal switch, "started in Ontario locations in May of 2024... set to roll out nationwide by the end of 2024. The company did issue a memo to franchisees"); concession 25 w ("several other Canadian Fried Chicken chains had already been offering halal chicken"); ingredient list 100 w; closer 38 w ("The original recipe claim is true at the ingredient level... What has changed quietly over the last two decades is the supply chain that feeds the original recipe. The Colonel never mentioned that."). This beat is the only one in either chicken video with an ownership line, a dated corporate event, a documented company action and a concession; it is the template beat.

Ask (67 w at 62.0%): "If this investigation is changing the way you think about your next fried chicken order, hit the subscribe button right now. Every subscriber tells YouTube that Canadians want to know..." Then "Now, let us get to the absolute worst offender."

Turn (146 w at 73.9%): "So, where does this leave the Canadian customer who actually wants fried chicken...? The good news is there are still two chains worth your money. Before we get to them, here is a simple observation" (the three-ingredient rule).

Close (143 w): summary ("Seven Canadian fried chicken chains hiding behind decades of marketing, industrial chemistry, foreign ownership..."), share ask, subscribe ask, comment ask ("Leave a comment telling me which Canadian chain should be investigated next"), sign-off "read your labels, question the claims, and protect what you put on your family's plate." No disclaimer.

### 1d. Broken Canada, "Tim Hortons and 9 Other Coffee Chains in Canada Just Got Caught" (Sep 2026, 162K, 7.2x median)

Cold open 101 w (2.2%): the felt-but-denied experience ("It doesn't taste the same. They're told they're imagining it. They're not."), then the promise in three parts: "exactly what changed inside your cup, which 10 chains are responsible, and the one rule that will make sure you never pay $4 for flavored water and corn syrup again." No ask.

Structure is chaptered with spoken chapter titles, and the list is only 20% of the words:
- Ch1 What coffee used to be 399 w (8.7%)
- Ch2 The corporate takeover nobody talked about 506 w (11.1%)
- Ch3 What they actually changed inside your cup 607 w (13.3%)
- Ranking intro 29 w, then #10 to #1 totalling 1,034 w (22.6%), starting at 35.9%: Tim Hortons 107, Second Cup 67, McCafe 104, Starbucks 134, Country Style 90, Robin's 82, Van Houtte 140, Timothy's 99, Presse Cafe 82, "the system itself" 129
- Ch5 The science of why you feel it but can't name it 442 w (9.7%)
- Ch6 The regulatory gap nobody filled 410 w (9.0%)
- Ch7 What honest coffee actually looks like 564 w (12.3%), names seven independent roasters
- Ch8 The one rule to remember + close 485 w (10.6%), starting 89.4%

Count and order: "ranked by how bad it got... starting with the worst offenders and working toward the ones that still have some integrity left," then inverts at #1: "number one on this list means the most egregious, not the best. Number one is the system itself." Stated on screen, then subverted.

List items are 67–140 words each: a one-line recognition, a one-line mechanism, a one-line closer. Example, McCafe: concession first ("To be fair to McDonald's, they never claimed to be a specialty coffee company"), then the problem (the 2010s premium pivot), then the closer ("That is not premium coffee. That is a vending machine with better lighting.").

Ask: none. There is no subscribe, like, share or comment ask anywhere in 4,577 words. Concession at 96%: "And listen, I go through the drive-thru, too... Some mornings the independent roaster is 4 km in the wrong direction and the Tim Hortons is right there. I understand the pull."

Close: a portable rule stated as a pair ("Check the roast date, check the origin. That's it. That's the whole rule."), the moral ("The brands that built their reputations on honest products sold those reputations to holding companies that saw a margin opportunity and took it. You felt the change before anyone admitted it was happening. You were right."), and the rule repeated as the final line.

Lesson for the redo: this outlier wins with a mechanism explainer, not a countdown, so its skeleton is not the one to copy; what transfers is (a) a stated ranking basis on screen, (b) a named-alternatives block with a price comparison ("One large Tim Hortons double-double per day is approximately 90 dollars per month... 25 dollars in whole beans... 15 and 20 cups"), (c) a first-person concession before the close, (d) a two-part rule the viewer can carry out of the video.

### 1e. Bagholder Association, Doritos (980K)

Cold open 91 w (3.9%): a dated, specific, verifiable event as the first sentence ("In early 2024, more than 12,000 supermarkets belonging to major retail chains from across Europe all removed Doritos from their shelves at the same time"), image of the empty shelf, then the question ("Why did Doritos, one of America's most beloved national snacks, collapse even after its most loyal customers turned their back on it?").

Single-subject arc, not a list:
- Origin and rise 326 w (13.9%), 1964 Disneyland to 1972 nacho cheese
- Cultural peak and the seed of hubris 139 w (5.9%): "PepsiCo's leadership began to believe that the brand was immortal, and that belief gave the company... the freedom to raise prices."
- The turn 186 w (8.0%), starting 23.8%: "Everything began sliding out of control during the brutal years of 2020 and 2021... They chose the second option."
- Shrinkflation and the spreadsheet logic 246 w (10.5%): "In 2022, observant customers discovered that Doritos family bags had been reduced from 9.75 oz to 9.25 oz."
- Backlash 333 w (14.2%): r/shrinkflation, TikTok, private label at $2.50–$3, Takis
- Financial consequence 118 w (5.0%): "For the first time in more than 13 years, PepsiCo's actual revenue recorded negative growth. The corporation missed its revenue target by more than $1 billion."
- Retailer war 210 w (9.0%): Carrefour January 2024, Edeka, Aldi, Kaufland, Walmart
- Activist and restructuring 145 w (6.2%): Elliott September 2025, $4 billion stake
- Surrender and recovery 255 w (10.9%): "Then, in February 2026, the company executed its most embarrassing surrender... cutting retail prices by up to 15%"
- Moral 289 w (12.4%), starting 87.6%: "Doritos had never started tasting worse... From beginning to end, there was only one problem, the unreasonable price created by greed."

Ask: none. Comment ask: none. The dated event chain is the whole video: 2020–21, 2021–23, 2022, 2023, January 2024, 2024, September 2025, late 2025, February 2026, Q1–Q2 2026. Every consequence is paired with a number.

### 1f. Bagholder Association, Pepsi (910K)

Cold open 123 w (4.8%): the fall stated as a ranking with dates ("Between 2023 and 2024, Dr. Pepper passed Pepsi... Then, Sprite overtook it, too, pushing Pepsi all the way down to fourth place... 8.3% less than half of its 18.8% peak back in 1984"), then "So what exactly happened?"

Arc: rise 727 w (28.4%) in two parts; hubris thesis stated in one sentence ("We can make Americans want whatever Pepsi says is cool") then four case studies, Crystal Pepsi 426 w, Project Blue 235 w, Refresh Project 136 w, Jenner 177 w; CONCESSION 207 w (8.1%) at 71.2% ("At this point, let's take a pause and look at the situation fairly. Pepsi burning money on massive media pushes only to stumble is a clear fact. But was it truly all self-inflicted?"); the numbers 59 w; rebuttal 170 w ("But this decline cannot simply be blamed on PepsiCo's product diversification. Just take a look at their competitors. Coca-Cola diversified, too."); close 300 w (11.7%) at 88.3%. No ask of any kind.

### 1g. Bagholder Association, Krispy Kreme (464K)

Cold open 106 w (4.2%): love, then two numbers ("Over 240 stores in the US had to close down... still carries nearly $550 million in net debt"), then the question.

Arc: origin 179 w; standardization 317 w; IPO and bonus mechanics 321 w (12.6%) at 23.6% (the greed mechanism: "executive bonuses were closely linked to and dependent upon these three specific metrics"); decline 413 w (16.2%) with company's own admission ("Krispy Kreme later directly acknowledged that the high initial sales levels at many stores could not be maintained"); Wall Street and SEC 215 w ("In May 2004, Krispy Kreme lowered its earnings guidance by roughly 10%. Its stock fell about 29% in a single day... The SEC later identified several violations"); JAB 191 w; McDonald's partnership 379 w; consequence 200 w ("By the end of fiscal 2025, Krispy Kreme reported a net loss of about 523.8 million dollars, while its net leverage ratio reached 6.7 times"); retrenchment and lesson 226 w at 91.1%. No ask.

Cross-format observation: the three formats that outperform the February video (Jan chicken 105K, BC 162K, BA 464K–980K) each carry fewer asks than it does, not more. BC and BA carry zero. Jan carries two (open and turn). The new script should carry exactly one mid-list ask plus one comment ask in the close, which is the February form.

---

## 2. Beat anatomy the new script must follow

Each of the seven chain beats runs eight jobs in this order. The line quoted under each job is the model to imitate, taken from the transcripts where the job is done best.

1. Recognition hook (40–60 w). The chain located in the viewer's own memory, one concrete Canadian detail, one dated founding fact.
   - "The phrase double-double became part of the Canadian vocabulary." (Feb, Tim Hortons)
   - "The red and white bucket has appeared at family dinners, road trips, and movie nights for multiple generations of Canadian families." (May, KFC)
   - "Customers lined up for fresh glazed donuts, and new store openings became major events." (BA, Krispy Kreme)

2. Ownership from filings (30–50 w). Who owns it, since when, for how much, and where the decisions are made. Name the parent, the largest shareholder, the deal value and the year. This is the beat's licence to say "corporate."
   - "In 2014, Restaurant Brands International acquired Tim Hortons in a deal worth approximately 11.4 billion dollars. Restaurant Brands International is the holding company that also owns Burger King and later Popeyes. Their largest shareholder is 3G Capital, a Brazilian private equity firm." (BC)
   - "The brand is now owned by Yum Brands, the same American corporation that operates Pizza Hut and Taco Bell headquartered in Louisville, Kentucky. The Colonel on the bucket is American. The corporate decisions about your Canadian chicken are made in Louisville." (May, KFC)
   - "In 2016, JAB Holding, the owner of the famous Dr. Pepper brand, bought Krispy Kreme for about 1.35 billion dollars." (BA)
   - "In 2024, Subway was sold to private equity firm Roark Capital for $9.6 billion." (Feb)

3. The dated event chain, in order (100–150 w). Three to five events, each opening with a month and year, each attributable to a filing, a court docket, a regulator or a named outlet. This is where Bagholder spends most of its words and where the channel's chicken videos spend none.
   - "In January 2024, the French retail giant Carrefour, with more than 12,000 stores across Europe, launched a direct punishment... By September 2025, the aggressive activist hedge fund Elliott Management swooped in, acquiring a massive $4 billion stake... Then, in February 2026, the company executed its most embarrassing surrender." (BA, Doritos)
   - "In May 2004, Krispy Kreme lowered its earnings guidance by roughly 10%. Its stock fell about 29% in a single day." (BA)
   - "In September 2025, Starbucks announced a $1 restructuring plan. According to CBC News, the company laid off approximately 900 non-retail employees and closed stores across Canada and the United States. In British Columbia alone, at least 12 locations closed over a single weekend." (Feb; fix the dollar figure, see section 5)
   - "The change started in Ontario locations in May of 2024 with Thunder Bay and Ottawa excluded and was set to roll out nationwide by the end of 2024. The company did issue a memo to franchisees and partnered publicly with Maple Lodge Farms." (May, KFC)

4. The company's own explanation, quoted (40–70 w). Let the company speak in its own filing or statement language, then sit on the phrase.
   - "According to the company's own financial disclosures, Boston Pizza restaurants were challenged in 2024 and early 2025 by impacts of inflation and interest rates, rising input costs, decreased labor availability, changes in consumer demand, and guest willingness to accept price increases." (Feb)
   - "The company said it was identifying stores that could not create the physical environment customers expect or that did not show a path to financial performance." (Feb, Starbucks)
   - "Tim Hortons confirmed it publicly in 2015, framing it as a quality consistency initiative. Quality consistency. That is the phrase they used. Let that sit for a moment." (BC)
   - "McDonald's stated the program met the expectations of their brand and franchisees. However, Charlesworth noted that efforts to align Krispy Kreme's costs with customer demand at each location had failed." (BA)
   - "Executives publicly declared they had spent the past year listening closely to consumers and understood their financial strain." (BA, Doritos)

5. The consequence, stated with its source (50–80 w). A number, a date, a document. Unit counts, closures, same-store sales, a court outcome, a settlement figure.
   - "The franchise sales from closed restaurants in 2024 were $7.4 million compared to just $3.4 million from the new restaurants that opened. This created a negative $4 million impact on franchise sales." (Feb, Boston Pizza, from the Royalties Income Fund MD&A)
   - "For the first time in more than 13 years, PepsiCo's actual revenue recorded negative growth. The corporation missed its revenue target by more than $1 billion." (BA)
   - "By the end of fiscal 2025, Krispy Kreme reported a net loss of about 523.8 million dollars, while its net leverage ratio reached 6.7 times." (BA)
   - "According to a Leger survey, a Tim Hortons reputational brand ranking plummeted from fourth place among Canadians in 2016 to 50th place just 2 years later." (Feb; add the survey year)

6. A concession (20–40 w). One sentence that gives the company or the counter-argument its due. It is what makes the next sentence land.
   - "Although the decisions were made at the franchise level, many customers blamed the corporate leadership." (Feb, Tim Hortons)
   - "Whether or not Wendy's truly intended to implement surge pricing, the damage was done." (Feb)
   - "To be fair to McDonald's, they never claimed to be a specialty coffee company. They claimed to be convenient and affordable, and on those metrics, McCafe largely delivers." (BC)
   - "It is worth noting that several other Canadian Fried Chicken chains had already been offering halal chicken at most of their locations for years before KFC made the switch." (May)
   - "Management pointed to the popularity of low-carbohydrate diets as one reason customers were buying fewer donuts. That may have contributed to the decline, but Krispy Kreme was already dealing with a much broader problem." (BA)
   - "At this point, let's take a pause and look at the situation fairly." (BA, Pepsi)

7. A named closer (15–35 w). One or two sentences that restate the beat as a reversal, using the chain's own promise against it.
   - "The company that built its fortune on being affordable had priced itself into a crisis." (Feb, McDonald's)
   - "The sandwich giant that once seemed unstoppable is now quietly collapsing one store at a time." (Feb, Subway)
   - "That is not premium coffee. That is a vending machine with better lighting." (BC)
   - "The name is on the door. The craft is not." (BC, Van Houtte)
   - "The original cooking method is true. What has changed quietly over the last two decades is the supply chain that feeds the original recipe. The Colonel never mentioned that." (May)
   - "Doritos is no longer selling tortilla chips. It is selling cheese-flavored air." (BA, quoting a commenter)

8. A bridge (10–25 w). Number, one-line characterization, name last.
   - "Halfway through our list, we arrive at a chain that made headlines for all the wrong reasons. Wendy's." (Feb)
   - "Number four brings us to a sandwich chain that was once the largest restaurant chain in North America. Subway." (Feb)
   - "Coming in at number two is a Canadian institution that has been quietly retreating across the country, Swiss Chalet." (Feb)
   - "Sitting at the next position is the chain that grew faster across Canada than any other fried chicken brand in the last decade." (May)
   - "And then, an opportunity appeared that could accelerate that plan almost instantly. McDonald's." (BA)

Order rule inside the beat: recognition, ownership, event chain, company explanation, consequence, concession, closer, bridge. The concession may move up to sit between the event chain and the explanation when the company's explanation is itself the concession (Boston Pizza pattern). Never open a beat with a complaint or a price.

---

## 3. House-rule breaches in the four Canadian transcripts, with the substitute device

Rules: (a) no health, nutrition or safety commentary; (b) no unattributed corporate claims; (c) no review-site quotes; (d) no "according to analysts" without a name; (e) no implied wrongdoing beyond the documented record, allegation vs finding vs settlement kept distinct; (f) prices with source and date; (g) no "we tested."

### 3a. February greedy restaurants (the redo's own predecessor)

Rule (d), unnamed analysts and aggregated sources:
- "According to industry analysts and franchise owners, Restaurant Brands International's approach relied heavily on aggressive cost reduction." Substitute: cite the Great White North Franchisee Association's 2017 statement of claim in Ontario Superior Court, or RBI's own 2015 annual report language on cost synergies, and name the outlet that reported the franchisee allegations (Globe and Mail, Financial Post).
- "But according to multiple reports and price tracking analyses, the company pushed prices so far..." and "According to financial analyses, McDonald's prices have increased substantially." Substitute: Statistics Canada CPI, food purchased from restaurants, with the index dates; or a named tracker with its date.
- "But analysts say the damage may already be done." Substitute: cut, or name the analyst, firm and date of the note.
- "But according to multiple financial reports and news sources, the combination of aggressive price increases, global controversies, and internal turmoil sent the company into a tailspin." Substitute: Starbucks Q4 FY2025 earnings release, dated.
- "According to analysts, the chain's struggles reflect a broader problem." Substitute: cut; the six-quarters figure carries the point on its own.
- "According to Global News, there were initially no plans to introduce the system in Canada, but experts predicted it was only a matter of time." Substitute: keep the Global News line, cut "experts predicted."
- "According to reports, Starbucks employs about 20,000 people in Canada." and "According to reports, the chain has faced significant challenges in maintaining its footprint." Substitute: the company's Canadian careers page or annual report, or cut.
- "According to the interim CEO, these locations are a drag." Substitute: name him (Ken Cook) and the earnings call date.
- "The franchise association said Subway has imposed a non-negotiable remodel timeline." Substitute: name it (North American Association of Subway Franchisees) and the date of the statement.

Rule (c), review-site quotes:
- Swiss Chalet: "Customer reviews paint a troubling picture of declining quality and value. According to reviews on Trustpilot and Tripadvisor, customers complain about increased prices, decreased quality, and poor service. Comments describe dried-out chicken, inedible fries, tiny portions... One reviewer wrote that the festive special was a joke and an insult, describing a tiny two-bite portion of dressing. Another noted that prices have increased while quality has decreased." (79 words.) Substitute: Recipe Unlimited's last public MD&A before the 2022 Fairfax take-private, which reports Swiss Chalet system sales and unit counts by year; the province-by-province closure record already in the beat (Burnaby August 2022, Kelowna, Victoria, Port Coquitlam) is the documented consequence.
- Boston Pizza: "Customer reviews reflect frustration with pricing. According to TripAdvisor reviews, those customers describe the chain as far too expensive and complain that it is not fine dining, yet charges fine dining prices." Substitute: the Royalties Income Fund MD&A is already quoted in the same beat and includes "guest willingness to accept price increases," which says the same thing in the company's own words.

Rule (f), prices without source or date:
- "A McChicken sandwich that cost $1 in 2018 now costs approximately $3.10 in 2025. A McDouble that cost $1.19 in 2014 now sells for $4.59. The average price of menu items increased approximately 40% between 2019 and 2024. McDonald's has raised prices at nearly double the rate of its rivals, including Burger King, Wendy's, and Harvey's." The $1 McChicken and $1.19 McDouble are US Dollar Menu figures; no Canadian source. Substitute: McDonald's Canada app price for a named item in a named city on a stated date, versus an archived menu (Wayback Machine) on a stated earlier date.
- "Viral posts about $18 Big Mac meals spread across every platform." US (Connecticut rest stop, 2023). Substitute: cut.
- "What used to cost $5 now costs nearly double at many locations." Substitute: Subway Canada app price, city, date.
- "A typical meal for two at Boston Pizza now costs between 70 and 90 dollars or more, including drinks, tax, and tip. Pasta dishes cost approximately $24. A personal pizza runs around 16 to 18 dollars." and "When a casual pizza dinner for four can easily exceed $150." Substitute: the posted menu price of two named items, city, date; drop the "meal for two/four" arithmetic or show it.
- "In October 2025, Tim Hortons raised coffee prices for the first time in 3 years, citing inflation pressures." Substitute: name the outlet that reported it and the size of the increase.
- "which increased sales volumes by roughly 2 and 1/2 million additional orders per month" (McDonald's $1 coffee). Substitute: source or cut.

Rule (b), unattributed corporate claims:
- "Longtime favorites like soups, baked goods, and sandwiches shifted to more centralized, frozen preparation to reduce costs." Substitute: Tim Hortons' own par-baking disclosure (the "Always Fresh" program, announced 2003) with date; note it predates RBI.
- "Customers insisted the coffee flavor had changed. Social media filled with complaints." Substitute: cut or replace with the Leger figure already present.
- "Traffic from lower-income consumers dropped nearly double digits." Substitute: attribute to the McDonald's CEO on the Q2 2024 earnings call by name and date.
- "Workers reported being offered transfers to locations more than 30 km away without compensation." Substitute: attribute to the Global News report already cited in the beat.
- Subway beat: "In Canada, the chain had a similar dominance," then every figure that follows (27,000 US locations, $490,000 average unit volume, 600 closures in 2024, 7,600 lost, 20,000 domestic, 28% reduction, 43-unit franchisee Chapter 11) is American. Substitute: Subway Canada unit counts from the Canadian franchise disclosure document or Restaurants Canada, by year.
- Wendy's beat: "Wendy's closed 140 locations in 2024" and "United States same-store sales fell by 4%" are US/global. Substitute: Wendy's Canadian unit count from the 10-K segment note, by year.

Rule (e), implied wrongdoing beyond the record:
- "But as we have shown you today, these companies made deliberate choices to prioritize profit over their customers." Intent is asserted, not shown. Substitute: "these companies' own filings describe the choices they made."
- "Then the greed set in. Subway abandoned the value pricing that made it famous." Substitute: the date the $5 footlong national promotion ended and the company statement that ended it.
- "According to CBC News, the incident revealed how restaurant chains are exploring new ways to extract more money from customers." Editorial conclusion attributed to a newsroom. Substitute: quote what CBC actually reported (digital menu boards) and keep the conclusion in the channel's voice.
- Tim Hortons: "Multiple lawsuits followed." Substitute: name the two GWNFA suits, the court, the amounts claimed, and the outcome (dismissed, settled, ongoing), since allegation vs finding vs settlement must be distinct.
- "Shrinkflation is now widespread across the restaurant industry." Substitute: cut or cite.
- "According to Statistics Canada, restaurant price inflation has consistently outpaced grocery inflation." Not consistently true (grocery outpaced restaurants through much of 2022–23). Substitute: cite the specific twelve-month comparison and its date.

Rule (a): none in the February video; keep it that way.
Rule (g): no "we tested" in the February video.

### 3b. January chicken (105K)

The five avoid beats are almost entirely review-site material, so rule (c) covers most of the section. Every line below breaks (c) unless marked otherwise:
- "KFC Canada has a 1.6 star rating based on hundreds of customer reviews with consumers overwhelmingly dissatisfied. Only 7% of customer issues get resolved when people contact their customer service line."
- "One reviewer described biting into chicken that was so salty they couldn't eat it. Another said the pieces are now half the size... A family that spent $42 on a bucket found they had to throw out seven of the 12 pieces." (also rule f)
- "One review put it bluntly... the Colonel would be rolling in his grave." "reviewers say they can only taste salt now."
- Mary Brown's: "One reviewer who had been eating there for over 25 years... What used to be called the Big Mary sandwich now gets called the Little Mary by disappointed customers."
- St-Hubert: "One reviewer at a Montreal location described ordering a beef rib that was cooked until dead... drier than the Atacama Desert." "One reviewer at a Quebec City location described waiting 3 hours." "One reviewer noted that feeding a family chicken with hormones and antibiotics now costs almost $200." (also rule a, and factually wrong: hormones are not permitted in Canadian poultry)
- Church's: "Customer service at Church's is rated at just 1.5 out of 5 with only 4% of consumers reporting their issues were resolved." "Reviewers describe spending months trying to use Church's mobile app."
- Dixie Lee: "One customer reported paying $72 for an 18-piece meal... they noted seeing 13 pieces for $40 at Mary Brown's." (also rule f; the same $72/18-piece figure is used earlier for Mary Brown's, an internal contradiction) "One reviewer described arriving at a Dixie Lee location to find dirty tables."
- Popeyes: "On Google, Popeyes locations in Canada average around four stars out of five."
- Jollibee: "Reviewers consistently describe the chicken as piping hot, super crispy, and super juicy."
- Swiss Chalet: "Reviewers across different cities and provinces describe receiving the same reliable experience."
Substitute for all of the above: the beat structure in section 2. Where the point is portion or price, use a dated menu comparison with source. Where the point is service, use a regulator, a court record or a named outlet, or drop it.

Rule (a), health, nutrition, safety:
- "Hygiene concerns are equally troubling. Multiple reviewers have asked staff to put on gloves while handling food, especially when dealing with raw poultry. When customers have to remind employees about basic food safety practices, something has gone seriously wrong."
- "Raw and undercooked chicken is an alarming pattern. One customer in Abbotsford reported biting into a chicken thigh and having blood pour out... This is a food safety issue that could land someone in the hospital." "complaints about receiving bloody chicken."
- "Hygiene and facility maintenance are recurring themes. Multiple Toronto area locations have had restroom facilities reported as out of order... it raises serious questions about what conditions look like behind the counter."
- "Another reported receiving undercooked chicken in a six-piece meal."
- Popeyes: "Popeyes has eliminated antibiotics important to human medicine from their chicken supply chain... removing artificial colors, flavors, and preservatives... transitioning to 100% cage-free eggs."
- Swiss Chalet: "birds are raised without antibiotics, and fed vegetable grain diets." "Fresh, grain-fed, preservative-free chickens."
- Close: "Your stomach will thank you."
Substitute: if a safety matter is needed, it must be a public health unit closure order or a CFIA recall with a date and a link; otherwise cut. Positive sourcing claims stay only as "the company states on its website that..." with date.

Rule (b), unattributed corporate claims (marketing copy repeated as fact):
- "enjoys the second highest customer loyalty of any restaurant in Canada trailing only Tim Hortons" (no source).
- "Every piece of chicken is marinated for a full 12 hours in bold Louisiana seasonings before being freshly breaded and hand-battered in restaurant. This isn't a gimmick." (Popeyes marketing)
- "The quality commitments are substantial and verifiable." (verified by whom)
- "Jollibee announced plans to open 350 new locations across the US and Canada... With stores averaging $4.3 million in gross sales." (source and date needed; JFC is listed on the Philippine Stock Exchange and files publicly)
- "Before cooking, chicken is marinated for 24 hours in a proprietary blend of 23 herbs and spices." "just a teaspoon of salt." "The secret recipe hasn't changed in nearly seven decades." (Swiss Chalet marketing)
- "The parent company, Restaurant Brands International... giving them significant resources to maintain quality standards across locations."
Substitute: "according to the company's website as of [date]" or the filing, or cut.

Rule (e):
- "The franchise model has created a race to the bottom where individual locations cut every corner possible."
- "it signals a corporate culture that has stopped caring about individual customer experiences."
- "the chains definitely don't want you to see."
Substitute: state the documented fact and let the viewer infer.

Rule (f): "A standard combo meal now costs between 12 and $15 with an 18-piece bucket costing over $75." "A batter's box that used to be reasonable now costs over $21. Customers report paying over $66 for a six-piece order. One family spent $72 on an 18-piece meal." "$30 meal." "almost $200." "13 pieces for $40." All need item, city, source, date.

Rule (g): no literal "we tested," but "I'm constantly researching what's really going on" implies fieldwork not shown. Substitute: "we went through the filings."

Consistency flag: this video calls Swiss Chalet great; the February video ranks it #2 greedy. It calls Mary Brown's an avoid; the May video makes Mary Brown's the top recommendation. The redo must pick one position per chain and, if it reverses a prior verdict, say so in one sentence with the new evidence.

### 3c. May chicken (72K)

Rule (a) dominates; the cold open (270 w) and much of every beat is nutrition commentary:
- Whole cold open: "tartrazine, sunset yellow, or allura red... may affect activity and attention in children. Titanium dioxide, which Health Canada still permits, was banned outright... 2007 University of Southampton study published in The Lancet... a chemistry experiment dressed up as comfort food."
- KFC: "the Kentucky Fried Chicken seasoning blend contains monosodium glutamate... a list of dough conditioners, leavening agents, and preservatives."
- Popeyes: "a single Popeyes chicken sandwich contains around 1,400 mg of sodium, well over half the daily upper limit recommended by Health Canada... monosodium glutamate, soybean oil, dextrose, autolyzed yeast extract, disodium inosinate, disodium guanylate... glucose fructose... has no place in a savory bread product... sunset yellow and allura red, two of the three dyes that would require a behavioral warning label."
- Chick-fil-A: "over 1,400 mg of sodium, monosodium glutamate, soybean oil, and the same dough conditioner system." "The chemistry is largely identical."
- Jollibee: "glucose fructose, modified milk ingredients, monosodium glutamate... drives a glycemic load that is significantly higher than what most Canadian customers realize."
- Dixie Lee: "dough conditioners and preservatives consistent with industry standards."
- Chester's: "monosodium glutamate, multiple sodium-based flavor enhancers, and a high sodium content overall."
- Turn: "over-engineered into a chemistry experiment... find 25 different additives."
- Church's: "cleaner side item formulations... real butter."
- Mary Brown's: "The healthiest option and the strongest recommendation." "cleaner sourcing."
- Close: "industrial chemistry... what additives are sitting in the breading... read your labels."
Substitute: the greed thesis replaces the chemistry thesis entirely. The equivalent evidentiary move is the filing, not the ingredient panel.

Rule (b), unattributed supply-chain and operations claims:
- "the in-store signage and menu boards at the location level have not consistently disclosed the change to walk-in customers."
- "arrives at the location already cut, breaded in central facilities for some product lines, and frozen for shipping."
- "the implication of southern authenticity that the actual operations do not consistently deliver." "the chain now operates nearly 400 locations" (RBI's 10-K gives the number; cite it).
- "Chick-fil-A chicken product itself is sourced from American supply chains for most ingredients" (Chick-fil-A Canada states Canadian-raised chicken; the claim needs a source or a cut).
- "Atlantic Canadian customers have reported wildly different experiences across Dixie Lee locations in the same province within the same month."
- "The waffles are produced through commercial waffle batter operations and cooked in standard waffle equipment rather than being made from scratch."
- "The chicken sits in warming cabinets behind the counter for extended periods, sometimes hours." "deep fryers that often share cooking oil with other products."
- Church's: "fried in oil that is changed on a stricter schedule than the industry standard."
- Mary Brown's: "a proprietary seasoning blend that the company has refused to outsource," "oil management standards that exceed industry baseline requirements," "verified Canadian sourcing" (verified by whom), "approximately 300 locations," "independence has translated into operational decisions that prioritize food quality over margin optimization."
Substitute: for every operational claim, either the company's own statement dated, a franchise disclosure document, or a named report; otherwise cut.

Rule (e):
- "Customers who had bought the same product for 30 years were not always directly informed inside the restaurant that the meat preparation methodology had changed." Implies concealment; the beat itself says a memo and a public partnership announcement exist. Substitute: state the memo, the partner announcement and the date, then state what was and was not on the menu board with a photo and date if one exists.
- Chick-fil-A: "significant baggage... well-documented donations... organizations that hold positions on social issues." Off-thesis and political. Substitute: cut from a greed video.
- "the same private equity playbook" (RBI). Substitute: the specific RBI filing language.
- "The Filipino heritage marketing is genuine in cultural intent, but the actual food production operates at the same industrial scale." Fine as opinion; keep out of a filings video.

Rule (f): "chicken and waffle combinations often exceeding 20 Canadian dollars before tax." "generally running between 12 and 17 Canadian dollars." "generally running between 12 and 16 Canadian dollars." No item, city, source or date.

Rule (g): title "I Investigated" and body "this investigation" imply original testing that the script does not show. Substitute: "we went through seven companies' filings, court records and announcements."

Transcription errors to avoid inheriting: "the 2000s and 2000s"; "start Dawkins start C"; KFC arrival year given as 1953 here and 1953 (franchise) in January, versus 1955 first franchise here.

### 3d. Broken Canada coffee (162K), for reference only

Rule (a): the sodium passage ("If your morning coffee is making you thirstier rather than more alert, check the sodium content"), "enough glucose to power a small vehicle," and the entire Regulatory Gap chapter are nutrition commentary the channel would not run.
Rule (b): "Former franchise owners, speaking on condition of anonymity to Canadian business publications" (publications unnamed); "Second Cup's in-store roasting equipment... has largely been replaced by pre-roasted supply"; "McCafe... the roasting is contracted out"; "Country Style... supply agreements that have not been meaningfully reviewed in years"; "Several of the chains listed above source from overlapping supplier networks" (the #1 claim, unsourced); "Cream blend in several chain applications contains modified milk ingredients, sodium phosphate" (chains unnamed); Robusta blending is implied for every chain and shown for none.
Rule (d): "Industry lobby groups have consistently and successfully argued" (unnamed).
Rule (f): "A large coffee costs 35 cents" (1975, unsourced); "somewhere between 18 and 28 dollars"; "approximately 90 dollars per month."
Rule (e): "an industrial flavor system designed in a laboratory in New Jersey" (no chain, no lab).
Note also: BC gives the RBI deal as 11.4 billion (USD) and the February video as 12 and 1/2 billion Canadian; both are correct in their currency. The redo should say "approximately US$11.4 billion, about C$12.5 billion at the time."
Rules (c), (g): clean. No review sites, no "we tested," no analysts.

---

## 4. Recommended skeleton for the new script

Target 21,000–23,000 characters of plain spoken prose, no markdown, no headers in the body; at 6.5 chars/word that is 3,350–3,400 words. Countdown 7 to 1 (or 5 to 1 if research supports only five chains with a documented event chain). Order: fewest to most Canadian units.

On-screen basis for the order, spoken once in the cold open and shown as a lower-third for each beat: "Ranked by number of Canadian locations, fewest to most, using each company's most recent annual report, franchise disclosure document or corporate store locator as of [month year]." Show the unit count and the source on every bridge. This kills the "why is X above Y" comment before it starts, and the ascending count means the most familiar chain lands at #1 by rule rather than by the writer's opinion.

Eligibility rule for a chain to hold a slot: it must have at least three dated events from the documented record (filing, court docket, regulator decision, company announcement, named-outlet report) and at least one quotable company statement. A chain with only reviews and prices does not qualify, however greedy it looks. Candidates from the three chicken transcripts with a documentary trail: KFC Canada (Yum 10-K; 2024 halal memo and Maple Lodge announcement; Canadian unit count by year), Popeyes (RBI 10-K, Canadian unit count and pricing language), St-Hubert and Swiss Chalet (Recipe Unlimited MD&A to 2022, Fairfax annual reports after; BC exit 2022), Church's (High Bluff Capital acquisition 2021; Canadian unit trend), Jollibee (JFC annual reports, PSE-listed; North America expansion statements), Chick-fil-A Canada (private; unit count from its own locator; thin on filings, likely a drop), Dixie Lee, Chester's, Cluck Clucks, Mary Brown's (all private; only qualify if a court record or franchise dispute exists). If fewer than seven qualify, run five and change the title count.

Word budgets (3,370 words, about 21,900 characters):

Cold open, 130 w (3.9%). Moves in order: (1) one universal scene, second person, one sentence, in the Feb form ("Every single day, millions of Canadians..."); (2) the reversal, "They wanted more"; (3) the triplet of sins, all three provable from filings (raised prices, closed stores, fought their own franchisees or customers in court); (4) the consequence promise with two categories that will each appear in the list (closures, court records); (5) the count and the order basis, spoken: "Seven chains, ranked from the fewest Canadian locations to the most, using their own filings." No subscribe ask in the open. Cold open must open on a dated fact, Bagholder-style, if research yields one strong enough (for example a month-and-year closure count or a court filing date).

#7, 280 w. Recognition 40, ownership 35, event chain 90, company explanation 40, consequence 40, concession 15, closer 20. Bridge to #6 at the end, 15 w, with unit count and source.
#6, 300 w. Same shape, event chain 100.
#5, 320 w. Event chain 110.
#4, 340 w. Event chain 120.
#3, 360 w. Event chain 130.
Mid-list ask, 55 w, placed here so it lands at 61–63% of words, the band all three channel videos used. Keep the February wording shape: "If you are getting value from this, take a second to subscribe and drop a comment below. Tell us which of these chains surprised you the most. Now, the final two." One ask only; this is the only interruption in the list.
#2, 380 w. Event chain 140.
#1, 420 w. Event chain 150, consequence 60. #1 is the chain with the most Canadian units, so the closer must say so: the biggest footprint, the most Canadians affected, the most to answer for.

Bridge to the alternatives, 50 w. Feb had no alternatives; borrow the May turn shape: "So where does this leave the Canadian customer who still wants fried chicken? There are two chains whose filings and public record do not show the pattern above." Disclosure sentence here, not later: "We have no commercial relationship with any chain named in this video, and none of them knew this video was being made."

Two or three worth-it alternatives, 170 w each if two (340) or 120 w each if three (360). Each alternative gets the same eight jobs compressed: recognition, ownership, what the record shows (stable or growing Canadian unit count with source and date, no franchisee litigation on the docket, a dated price freeze or value announcement if one exists), one concession (what the record does not tell us), one named closer. No marketing copy repeated as fact; "the company states on its website as of [date]" is the only permitted form. No health or sourcing virtues. The alternative's claim to be worth it rests on price with source and date, unit growth, and the absence of the documented pattern, nothing else.

How to protect yourself, 190 w, in the February form: four numbered actions, each two to four sentences, opening "First," "Second," "Third," "Fourth." Recommended four: First, vote with your wallet (keep the Feb line "These companies respond to one thing and one thing only, revenue"). Second, check the price before you order, with the device the viewer can use (the chain's own app shows the price for your postal code; screenshot it and compare next month). Third, read the franchise disclosure or annual report for any chain you eat at weekly; they are public and they say in their own words what the chain expects you to accept. Fourth, support the chains and independents whose record is clean, with the Statistics Canada restaurant-versus-grocery comparison quoted with its date.

Moral and comment ask, 70 w. The Feb triplet: "These seven chains had something in common. They all forgot a simple truth. The customer always has a choice." Then the share ask and one comment ask ("leave a comment telling us which chain on this list shocked you the most," or "which Canadian chain should be investigated next"). Then the tagline "the truth is not always on the menu."

Disclaimer, 45 w, spoken as the final lines and shown on screen: "Everything in this video comes from public filings, court records, regulator decisions, company statements and named news reports as of [date]. Where a matter is an allegation, we have called it an allegation. Prices quoted are from [source] on [date] and vary by location."

Total: 130 + 280 + 300 + 320 + 340 + 360 + 55 + 380 + 420 + 50 + 350 + 190 + 70 + 45 = 3,290 words, about 21,400 characters; the event chains can absorb the remaining headroom to 22,500.

Vocabulary rules for the body: every event opens with month and year; every number is followed by its source in the same sentence ("according to the Boston Pizza Royalties Income Fund's 2024 management discussion and analysis"); "alleged" for anything in a statement of claim, "found" only for a court or regulator decision, "settled" with the amount and date and the words "without admission" if that is what the settlement says; no "analysts," "experts," "reports," "reviews," "customers say," "social media exploded"; no "we tested," "we visited," "we tasted"; no ingredient, sodium, additive, hygiene or undercooked language at all.

---

## 5. Exact February lines the audience could challenge (do not repeat)

Unsourced or wrongly sourced numbers:
1. "A McChicken sandwich that cost $1 in 2018 now costs approximately $3.10 in 2025. A McDouble that cost $1.19 in 2014 now sells for $4.59." US Dollar Menu prices in a Canadian video, no source.
2. "The average price of menu items increased approximately 40% between 2019 and 2024. McDonald's has raised prices at nearly double the rate of its rivals, including Burger King, Wendy's, and Harvey's." No source.
3. "Viral posts about $18 Big Mac meals spread across every platform." US post.
4. "which increased sales volumes by roughly 2 and 1/2 million additional orders per month." No source.
5. "Starbucks announced a $1 restructuring plan." Should be $1 billion; the transcript says one dollar.
6. "According to reports, Starbucks employs about 20,000 people in Canada." Unnamed.
7. "Workers reported being offered transfers to locations more than 30 km away without compensation." Unattributed.
8. "What used to cost $5 now costs nearly double at many locations." No source, date or item.
9. "To put that in perspective, the average Chick-fil-A generates over $8 million per location." US figure; Chick-fil-A had a handful of Canadian units.
10. The entire Subway consequence block, "Subway closed over 600 locations in 2024 alone... lost approximately 7,600 locations... dropped below 20,000 domestic restaurants... 28% reduction in its American footprint," is US data under a sentence that says "In Canada, the chain had a similar dominance." No Canadian unit count is ever given.
11. "Wendy's closed 140 locations in 2024" and "In the first 9 months of 2025, United States same-store sales fell by 4%." US data, no Canadian figure.
12. "In October 2025, Tim Hortons raised coffee prices for the first time in 3 years, citing inflation pressures." No outlet, no amount.
13. "A Maclean's survey revealed that Tim Hortons is no longer the favorite coffee shop in Canada, trailing behind McDonald's, Second Cup, and Starbucks." Undated.
14. "A typical meal for two at Boston Pizza now costs between 70 and 90 dollars or more, including drinks, tax, and tip. Pasta dishes cost approximately $24. A personal pizza runs around 16 to 18 dollars." and "When a casual pizza dinner for four can easily exceed $150." No source, item, city or date.
15. "According to Statistics Canada, restaurant price inflation has consistently outpaced grocery inflation." Not consistently true for 2022–23; needs the specific period.
16. "Shrinkflation is now widespread across the restaurant industry." No source.

Review-site quotes:
17. "According to reviews on Trustpilot and Tripadvisor, customers complain about increased prices, decreased quality, and poor service. Comments describe dried-out chicken, inedible fries, tiny portions... One reviewer wrote that the festive special was a joke and an insult, describing a tiny two-bite portion of dressing. Another noted that prices have increased while quality has decreased." (Swiss Chalet)
18. "According to TripAdvisor reviews, those customers describe the chain as far too expensive and complain that it is not fine dining, yet charges fine dining prices." (Boston Pizza)

"Analysts say" and unnamed aggregates:
19. "According to industry analysts and franchise owners, Restaurant Brands International's approach relied heavily on aggressive cost reduction."
20. "But according to multiple reports and price tracking analyses, the company pushed prices so far that customers finally pushed back."
21. "According to financial analyses, McDonald's prices have increased substantially over the past decade."
22. "But analysts say the damage may already be done."
23. "But according to multiple financial reports and news sources, the combination of aggressive price increases, global controversies, and internal turmoil sent the company into a tailspin."
24. "According to analysts, the chain's struggles reflect a broader problem."
25. "experts predicted it was only a matter of time." (Wendy's surge pricing in Canada)
26. "According to reports, the chain has faced significant challenges in maintaining its footprint across the country." (Swiss Chalet)
27. "According to the interim CEO" (unnamed) and "The franchise association said" (unnamed).

Implied wrongdoing beyond the record:
28. "Multiple lawsuits followed." Which, where, outcome.
29. "Then the greed set in. Subway abandoned the value pricing that made it famous."
30. "According to CBC News, the incident revealed how restaurant chains are exploring new ways to extract more money from customers." Editorial conclusion attributed to CBC.
31. "But as we have shown you today, these companies made deliberate choices to prioritize profit over their customers." Intent asserted for seven companies on the strength of two MD&A quotes.
32. "Customers insisted the coffee flavor had changed. Social media filled with complaints about declining consistency and smaller portion sizes." Unattributed.

Structural challenge:
33. No stated basis for the 7-to-1 order, and #1 ("the restaurant that exemplifies how Canadian dining chains have lost touch") is the writer's judgment. The redo's ascending-unit-count basis answers this.
34. Two chains use the same source type twice (Recipe Unlimited owns Swiss Chalet; the Boston Pizza MD&A is quoted twice) while McDonald's and Subway have no company statement at all. Every beat in the redo carries one.

What February did right and the redo keeps: the Boston Pizza beat's MD&A quotes and the $7.4 million versus $3.4 million closure arithmetic; the Starbucks beat's CBC and Global News attributions and the "physical environment customers expect" company quote; the Tim Hortons beat's Leger and Gustavson survey citations and the franchise-level concession; the four-step "How to protect yourself" close; the mid-list ask at 63% doubling as the comment prompt; the "truth is not always on the menu" tagline.

# Popeyes Louisiana Kitchen — Canada dossier

Prepared 27 September 2026 for a Canadian consumer-investigation channel. Every URL below was opened on 27 Sep 2026 unless marked otherwise ("could not open" = blocked/403/429 on that date; such sources are NOT relied on). Tiers: (a) primary; (b) named outlet, byline + date; (c) aggregator/blog/SEO — flagged only, never cited; (d) documented consumer/media record.

House-rule reminders applied throughout: no health/food-safety commentary; every legal item labelled allegation / finding / settlement / admission / company statement / pending; corporate claims attributed; prices only from company channels or dated (b) reports; store counts only from filings, company releases or (b) reports with exact wording.

---

## 1 Ownership and Canadian operator

| Fact | Source (tier, opened 27 Sep 2026) |
|---|---|
| Ultimate parent: **Restaurant Brands International Inc.** ("RBI"), a Canadian corporation (NYSE/TSX: QSR), indirect holding company for Tim Hortons, Burger King, Popeyes and Firehouse Subs. Principal executive office 5707 Waterford District Drive, Miami. | RBI Form 10-K FY2025, filed Feb 2026 — https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1618756/000161875626000017/qsr-20251231.htm (a) |
| RBI acquired Popeyes Louisiana Kitchen, Inc. and subsidiaries on **27 March 2017**. | RBI 10-K FY2019 (a) — https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1618756/000161875620000004/qsr2019123110k.htm (surfaced via search; content confirmed in search summary of filing) |
| Franchisor / trademark owner: **Popeyes Louisiana Kitchen, Inc.** ("Popeyes® and Popeyes Louisiana Kitchen® are trademarks of Popeyes Louisiana Kitchen, Inc.") Canadian releases are issued by "Popeyes Louisiana Kitchen, Inc." and quote a "General Manager, Popeyes Canada" / "General Manager of Popeyes® Louisiana Kitchen Canada". | RBI 10-K FY2025 (a); CNW releases 14 Sep 2020 and 25 Oct 2022 (a) — https://www.newswire.ca/news-releases/the-wait-is-over-popeyes-r-brings-the-chicken-sandwich-that-broke-the-internet-to-canada-on-september-14-868890614.html ; https://www.newswire.ca/news-releases/popeyes-r-opens-its-300th-restaurant-in-canada-continuing-the-brand-s-rapid-growth-across-the-country-883087345.html |
| Reporting structure: RBI's **"PLK" segment = "Operations of our Popeyes brand in the U.S. and Canada"**. RBI does **not** publish a Canada-only Popeyes restaurant count or Canada-only Popeyes comparable sales in its 10-K or quarterly releases. | RBI 10-K FY2025 (a), text grep of full filing on 27 Sep 2026 |
| Supply chain: "BK and PLK work with purchasing cooperatives that negotiate the purchase terms for most equipment and products… We do not control these purchasing cooperatives." In Canada the co-op named in court filings/press is **Restaurant Services Canada Inc. (RSCI)**. | RBI 10-K FY2025 (a); Globe and Mail, 26 Jul 2025 (b) — https://www.theglobeandmail.com/business/article-fowl-play-inside-a-chicken-processors-tangled-battle-with-popeyes/ |
| No master franchisee: Popeyes Canada is franchised directly by the RBI-owned franchisor to individual franchisees (multiple GTA franchisee corporations named in court records — see §5). The company says the first Canadian restaurant opened in Toronto in **1984**. | CNW release 25 Oct 2022 (a); Insolvency Insider 2 Nov 2025 (b) — https://insolvencyinsider.ca/p/bdo-appointed-interim-receiver-to-14-popeyes-franchise-corporations-tied-to-irfan-memon |
| Canadian leadership: **Rob Manuel**, General Manager, Popeyes Canada (quoted 2020–Jan 2025). **Brian Trier** named General Manager and **Lisa Mazurkewich** Sr. Director of Marketing, Popeyes Canada, 28 Apr 2025. **Matt Harper** quoted as Sr. Director of Marketing, Popeyes Canada, 13 Apr 2026. | Popeyes newsroom 28 Apr 2025 (a) — https://news.popeyes.com/blog-posts/new-leaders-join-popeyes-r-canada-to-accelerate-growth ; CNW 13 Apr 2026 (a) — https://www.newswire.ca/news-releases/popeyes-r-sets-sail-with-one-piece-with-an-epic-anime-inspired-menu-824420397.html |
| Brand leadership over Canada: **Jeff Klein**, President, Popeyes Canada and U.S. (quoted Apr 2025); replaced by **Peter Perdue**, "President of Popeyes, U.S. and Canada", announced 4 Nov 2025 ("appointed President of Popeyes U.S. & Canada in November 2025" per 10-K). Sami Siddiqui (now RBI CFO) was President, Popeyes U.S. & Canada from Sep 2020. | RBI release 4 Nov 2025 (a) — https://www.rbi.com/English/news/news-details/2025/RBI-Announces-New-President-of-Popeyes-and-Chief-Operating-Officer-of-Burger-King/default.aspx ; RBI 10-K FY2025 (a) |

Registry note: no provincial/federal corporate-registry lookup was completed (registries not machine-readable in this session). Legal names above come from SEC filings, court reporting and company releases only.

No Canada-only 2026 count exists at tier (a)/(b).

## 4 Documented events 2022–2026 (date order)

Each: date — what happened — who said it — tier — status.

1. **Mar 2022** — First Atlantic Canada restaurant opens (Halifax). Company later says demand caused a drive-through line "over a kilometre long". — Popeyes release; Huddle.Today — (a)/(b) — company statement.
2. **19 Apr 2022** — RBI says Popeyes will open "over 200 new restaurants in the U.S. and Canada this year" after 208 in 2021. — Restaurant Dive, Julie Littman (b) — https://www.restaurantdive.com/news/popeyes-will-open-over-200-units-in-us-and-canada-in-2022/622230/ — company statement.
3. **25 Oct 2022** — Company announces 300th Canadian restaurant. — CNW (a) — company statement.
4. **Late 2021 → 10 Dec 2024** — ADP Direct Poultry Ltd. (Etobicoke) supplies raw chicken to Popeyes Canada; supply relationship terminated by Restaurant Services Canada on 10 Dec 2024 (per Globe reporting of the claim and RBI statements). — Globe and Mail 5 Jun and 26 Jul 2025 (b) — allegation (ADP) / company statement (RBI).
5. **13 Feb 2024 / 15 Feb 2024** — RBI reports PLK FY2023 comparable sales +4.8%, and sets five-year target for Popeyes "from nearly 3,400 in 2023 to over 4,200 restaurants by 2028" (U.S. & Canada). — RBI releases (a) — company statement.
6. **12 Feb 2025** — RBI reports PLK FY2024 comparable sales **+0.4%**, Q4 2024 **−0.2%**; 3,520 PLK restaurants. — RBI release (a) — company disclosure.
7. **7 Jan 2025** — $4 Favourites Value Menu launched across Canada. — CNW (a) — company statement.
8. **Jan 2025** — Popeyes sends default notices to Irfan Memon's franchisee companies citing "repeated failures to pay their employees amounts owing when due for a period of more than two months" (per court/termination documents reported by the Globe). — Globe and Mail 23 Oct 2025 (b) — franchisor notice as reported; allegation against franchisee.
9. **28 Apr 2025** — Popeyes names new Canadian GM and marketing lead; Jeff Klein: "Canada represents a tremendous growth opportunity for Popeyes." — Popeyes newsroom (a) — company statement.
10. **May 2025 (claim issued 26 May 2025; Globe's later feature says "April 2025")** — **ADP Direct Poultry Ltd. v. Popeyes Louisiana Kitchen Inc., Restaurant Brands International Inc., Restaurant Services Canada Inc., Amjad Farooq Inc. and several franchisee corporations**, Ontario Superior Court of Justice. ADP seeks about $35–36 million from Popeyes/RBI/RSCI, $10–10.5 million from the alleged unauthorized supplier, $150,000 per franchisee, plus punitive/aggravated damages and $1 million for alleged defamation; alleges breach of contract, conspiracy, negligence and that franchisees bought chicken outside the approved supply chain. Named franchisee defendants include Y & F Food Corp. Ltd.; "24 restaurants in Greater Toronto Area and Brampton" implicated per the claim. — Globe and Mail, Susan Krashinsky Robertson, 5 Jun 2025 (b) — https://www.theglobeandmail.com/business/article-lawsuit-accuses-popeyes-of-selling-unsafe-chicken-in-ontario/ ; CP24, Alex Arsenych, 6 Jun 2025 (b) — https://www.cp24.com/news/2025/06/06/popeyes-accused-of-using-unsafe-chicken-allegedly-stored-in-residential-garages-in-ontario/ — **ALLEGATION; PENDING; not tested in court.** Company statement: Popeyes "previously investigated the food safety allegations they are making and found no evidence to support them."
11. **Jun 2025** — Popeyes/RBI informs Memon's companies of "significant discrepancies" between chicken purchased from approved suppliers and chicken sold (per the 18 Sep 2025 termination notice as reported). — Globe and Mail 23 Oct 2025 (b); Verdict Food Service 27 Oct 2025 (b, no byline — corroborative only) — franchisor allegation against franchisee.
12. **26 Jul 2025** — Globe feature "Fowl play": RBI spokesperson Emily Ciantra: "We have 380 restaurants in Canada"; ADP "highlighted between 3-15 restaurants in the GTA for us to look into" in 2022 and 2024; RBI reviewed "records of chicken purchases and sales by franchisees over a six-month period"; RBI calls the suit "a simple dispute about contract law" by "a former supplier who is lashing out after losing our business for a variety of legitimate reasons, including failing a majority of 165 product quality tests"; "We will hold them accountable through the legal process." Two franchisee companies had filed statements of defence denying the allegations; RBI had not yet filed a defence. — Globe (b) — company statements; allegations pending.
13. **18 Sep 2025** — Popeyes issues notice of termination to Memon's franchisee companies (reasons as reported: purchase/sales discrepancies without "sufficient or satisfactory" explanation; "the presence of rodent activity" at one Toronto location; unpaid wages). — Globe 23 Oct 2025 (b); Verdict 27 Oct 2025 (b) — franchisor action; allegations against franchisee.
14. **26 Sep 2025** — Employees protest at Memon-operated GTA locations alleging unpaid wages and bounced cheques; restaurants closed that night. — Globe and Mail, 29 Sep 2025 (b) — https://www.theglobeandmail.com/business/article-popeyes-franchisee-loses-restaurants-employees-unpaid-wages/ — allegation (employees).
15. **29 Sep 2025** — **RBI terminates franchise agreements for 13 Popeyes locations in and around the GTA** operated by Irfan Memon; restaurants "temporarily closed"; RBI says it is working to transfer them to other franchisees. RBI statement: "Treating restaurant team members with respect – including paying them on time – is fundamental to our brand's values. We are in touch with team members who have contacted us and are committed to getting them paid – optimally by forcing Mr. Memon to live up to his responsibilities." — Globe (b) — company action + statement.
16. **8–10 Oct 2025** — Meridian Credit Union Ltd. applies (8 Oct) and the **Ontario Superior Court of Justice (Commercial List) appoints BDO Canada Ltd. interim receiver (10 Oct 2025, on consent)** over 14 corporations that each operated one Popeyes restaurant: 2225909 Ontario Inc., 2397495 Ontario Ltd., 2619342 Ontario Inc., Al-Baasit Foods Inc., Al-Haadi Foods Inc., Al-Haqq Foods Inc., Al-Khaliq Foods Inc., Al-Mueed Foods Inc., Al-Razzaaq Foods Inc., Al-Waali Foods Inc., Al-Wakeel Foods Inc., An-Naafi Foods Inc., MIFK Foods Inc., Y & F Food Corporation Ltd. Outstanding credit approximately **$10.83 million** (as of 6 May 2025); unpaid wages, source deductions and HST remittances also cited; credit facilities advanced Aug 2023–Mar 2024; demands/BIA notices May 2025; forbearance Sep 2025; comeback hearing 12 Nov 2025. Seven of the 14 locations were named in the ADP lawsuit. — Globe and Mail 23 Oct 2025 (b) — https://www.theglobeandmail.com/business/article-popeyes-ontario-shut-down-unpaid-wages-lawsuit-unsafe-chicken/ ; Insolvency Insider, Dina Kovacevic, 2 Nov 2025 (b) — **court order (finding of insolvency proceedings); franchisee-level, not franchisor.**
17. **30 Oct 2025** — RBI Q3 2025: PLK comparable sales **−2.4%**; 3,541 restaurants. — RBI release (a) — company disclosure.
18. **4 Nov 2025** — RBI replaces Popeyes U.S. & Canada president (Klein → Perdue). Kobza: "Popeyes has the best chicken in QSR, and Peter's mandate is to unlock the share gains we know we are capable of." — RBI release (a) — company statement.
19. **19 Nov 2025** — Court grants ADP leave (unopposed) to amend its claim to add further corporate and individual defendants (reported in the 2026 decision summary; primary text not retrievable — see §9). — status: PENDING.
20. **12 Feb 2026** — RBI Q4/FY2025: PLK comparable sales **−4.8% Q4, −3.2% FY2025**; system-wide sales −0.7% FY; 3,578 restaurants; NRG 1.6%. Kobza on call: Popeyes "franchise profitability declined to roughly $235,000" (Q4 2025 vs $255,000 Q4 2024 per NRN); Patrick Doyle: that level "is not where the system can or should be"; field operations team increased "approximately 75%". — RBI release (a) — https://www.rbi.com/English/news/news-details/2026/Restaurant-Brands-International-Inc--Reports-Fourth-Quarter-and-Full-Year-2025-Results/default.aspx ; Nation's Restaurant News, Alicia Kelso, 13 Feb 2026 (b) — https://www.nrn.com/quick-service/popeyes-shifts-focus-to-operations-to-turn-around-negative-trends — company disclosure/admission of weak performance (segment = U.S.+Canada; RBI labels the comps "U.S.").
21. **Mar 2026 (decision reported as 2026 ONSC 1823)** — Ontario Superior Court dismisses a proposed defendant's cross-motion to strike paragraphs of ADP's supporting affidavit. — Aggregator summary only (minicounsel.ca returned 429; CanLII blocked) — see §9; **status PENDING**.
22. **13 Apr 2026** — ONE PIECE menu launched in Canada; Luffy Bento Bundle $9.99. — CNW (a) — company statement.
23. **6 May 2026** — RBI Q1 2026: PLK comparable sales **−6.5%**; system-wide sales −3.9%; 3,559 restaurants. — 8-K release (a) — company disclosure.
24. **6 Aug 2026** — RBI Q2 2026: PLK comparable sales **−5.1%** (U.S. −5.2%); system-wide sales −3.1%; 3,542 restaurants (+18 y/y). RBI says it expects Popeyes to "return to positive comparable sales in the second half of 2026". — 8-K release (a); CNBC 6 Aug 2026 (b, via search) — company disclosure/statement.

## 7 Consumer and media record (tier d only)

- **No named brand-trust or consumer-satisfaction survey** (with publisher, year and methodology) covering Popeyes Canada was located in this session. Searches for Léger, Gustavson Brand Trust Index and Canadian restaurant satisfaction rankings did not surface a Popeyes Canada entry at tier (d). Treat as "none found", not as absence.
- Media record that qualifies as documented (b), already in §4: Globe and Mail four-part coverage (Jun–Oct 2025, Susan Krashinsky Robertson); CP24/CTV (6 Jun 2025); Insolvency Insider (2 Nov 2025); Nation's Restaurant News (13 Feb 2026).
- Employee protests (26 Sep 2025) are documented by the Globe as events, but the underlying wage claims are allegations, not findings.
- Review-site complaints (Tripadvisor, Reddit, Trustpilot) — not counted, per brief.
