# INDUSTRY CONTEXT FILE — Canadian quick-service / fried-chicken segment
Chain: **context** (not a chain dossier). Verdict for script purposes: **NONE** (context only; no chain-specific "price paid" claim is made in this file).

Research date: all URLs below were opened on **27–28 September 2026** (session ran 27 Sep 2026 evening ET / 28 Sep 2026 UTC). "Opened" = fetched and read in this session. Tier labels: (a) primary; (b) named outlet with byline/date; (c) never cited; (d) documented consumer/media record.

Access notes that affect this file (recorded so the team knows what was and was not checked):
- Web search quota was exhausted before this task started; every source below was reached by direct URL or by an API/registry query, not by search engine.
- CanLII (all paths, incl. API), the Competition Tribunal website and its Decisia decisions database, the Ontario Court of Appeal Decisia database, the Federal Court Decisia database and the Quebec Registre des actions collectives all returned HTTP 403 to this session. The BC Courts judgment search failed TLS. Court records below therefore come from the Canadian Bar Association's National Class Action Database (which hosts the filed court documents as PDFs) — tier (a) for the documents themselves (they are the court filings), with the caveat that CBA participation is voluntary and the database is not exhaustive.
- Ontario e-Laws is JavaScript-only; the statute text was read from the Internet Archive's capture of the official e-Laws consolidation (capture 24 Jan 2025 of the page stating "current to November 1, 2024"). Both URLs are given.
- restaurantscanada.org returned 403 to command-line requests but was readable through the browser-style fetcher; Restaurants Canada's Cision newsroom (newswire.ca/news/restaurants-canada) was also used for its 24 Sep 2026 release.

Base-effect warning for the script (tier a): the Dec 2024 – Feb 2025 dip in restaurant CPI and the Dec 2025 – Feb 2026 spike are the federal GST/HST break, not a restaurant price cut/spike. StatCan, The Daily, "Consumer Price Index, January 2025", released 2025-02-18, https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/daily-quotidien/250218/dq250218a-eng.htm (opened 28 Sep 2026), verbatim: "Prices for food purchased from restaurants declined 5.1% year over year in January, which is more than triple the previous record decline of 1.6% in December 2024." and "Ahead of the tax break, a review of all representative products within the CPI was conducted to apply changes to taxation patterns starting on December 14, 2024." and "The impact of the GST/HST break will continue for the February CPI, as the tax exemption was applied until February 15, 2025."

Latest StatCan wording (tier a): The Daily, "Consumer Price Index, August 2026", released 2026-09-14, https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/daily-quotidien/260914/dq260914a-eng.htm (opened 28 Sep 2026), verbatim: "The Consumer Price Index (CPI) rose 3.0% year over year in August" ; food purchased from stores "rising 2.8% year over year" in August after "increasing 3.1% in July"; "For the first time since July 2024, grocery price growth increased at a slower pace than the all-items CPI in August 2026." (The August release text did not discuss restaurant prices; the +3.1% restaurant figure above is computed from the table.)

Cross-check (tier a): The Daily, "Food services and drinking places, June 2026", released 2026-08-26, https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/daily-quotidien/260826/dq260826c-eng.htm (opened 28 Sep 2026), verbatim: "Non-seasonally adjusted prices for food purchased from restaurants were up 2.7% in June compared with June 2025." — matches the +2.7% computed above for 2026-06.

## 2 Restaurants Canada — own published statements 2024–2026 (traffic, same-store sales, closures, profitability)
All tier (a) as the association's own releases/posts. Quotation marks = verbatim text as read today. Unquoted figures are as stated in the release but were read through a summarizing fetcher — re-open the URL before putting the exact sentence on screen. Restaurants Canada is a trade association; treat its survey figures as the association's own claims ("Restaurants Canada says").

**24 Sep 2026 — news release, "Eight in 10 Canadians are cutting back on restaurant visits as affordability pressures worsen"** (Foodservice Facts 2026 launch). Restaurants Canada newsroom on Cision: https://www.newswire.ca/news-releases/eight-in-10-canadians-are-cutting-back-on-restaurant-visits-as-affordability-pressures-worsen-887261515.html and own site https://www.restaurantscanada.org/eight-in-10-canadians-are-cutting-back-on-restaurant-visits-as-affordability-pressures-worsen/ (both opened 28 Sep 2026). Verbatim from the Cision listing: "Eight in 10 Canadians (80%) are eating out less often due to the rising cost of living, up from 75%". As stated in the release body (paraphrase): 80% of Canadians are reducing restaurant visits due to rising living costs, up from 75% a year earlier; 78% of households earning $100,000+ report cutting back vs 70% in 2025; approximately 24 million restaurant visits a day; restaurant sales projected to reach nearly $130 billion in 2026; average pre-tax profit margin 4.1%; 41% of restaurant companies operate at a loss or break even vs 12% in 2019; 1.2 million workers; $43 billion of food and beverage purchases, 68% from Canadian suppliers. The release does not give a same-store-sales or traffic-count series; "eating out less often" is a consumer survey response, not a transaction count.

**25 Sep 2026 — Economist's Notebook, "30 days of restaurant sales. One day of profit."** by Chris Elliott, Chief Economist and Vice President of Research, https://www.restaurantscanada.org/research/30-days-of-restaurant-sales-one-day-of-profit/ (opened 28 Sep 2026). Verbatim: "just one day out of the entire month as pre-tax profit". Illustrative model: days 1–10 cost of goods sold; 11–20 salaries, wages and payroll taxes; 21–22 rent and leasing; 23–29 operating costs; day 30 pre-tax profit. No external data source cited in the post.

**12 Aug 2026 — Economist's Notebook, "Navigating Canadians' Cost-of-Living Concerns and Rising Restaurant Operating Costs"** by Chris Elliott, https://www.restaurantscanada.org/research/restaurant-menu-price-growth-slows-as-consumers-prioritize-value/ (opened 28 Sep 2026). Verbatim: "restaurant menu prices rose at their slowest rate since the summer of 2021, increasing 2.7% in June compared with June 2025"; "Menu prices at full-service restaurants increased 3.3%—up from 3.0% in May"; "menu prices at quick-service restaurants rose just 1.7% year over year in June"; "half of quick-service restaurant guests say they are looking for promotions, discounts and deals"; "more than 8 in 10 Canadians say affordability is at least a moderate factor"; provincial menu inflation "ranging from a low of 1.9% in Newfoundland and Labrador to a high of 4.9% in Manitoba". (The 2.7% and 1.7% match StatCan CPI v41691046 and v41691048 for June 2026 in section 1.)

**20 Jul 2026 — news release, "Rising costs outpace sales growth, eroding restaurant profitability, new report finds"** (The Quarterly, Q2 2026), https://www.restaurantscanada.org/rising-costs-outpace-sales-growth-eroding-restaurant-profitability-new-report-finds/ (opened 28 Sep 2026). As stated: real (inflation-adjusted) commercial foodservice sales growth expected at 1.5% for 2026; 64% of operators report lower profitability than a year earlier; 41% of operators at a loss or breaking even, up from 36% in March; 73% cite tax policies as limiting investment; ~50,000 youth jobs added in H1 2026. Kelly Higginson, President and CEO (partial verbatim): "Stronger sales are always welcome, but they aren't enough to offset the cost pressures restau[rants face]". Members-only report page https://www.restaurantscanada.org/research/the-quarterly-q2-2026/ shows the teaser (verbatim): "Commercial foodservice sales increased by a stronger-than-expected 6.0%".

**20 May 2026 — Economist's Notebook, "How Rising Uncertainty is Impacting the Restaurant Industry"** by Sara Hamdy, Research Analyst, https://www.restaurantscanada.org/research/how-rising-uncertainty-is-impacting-the-restaurant-industry/ (opened 28 Sep 2026). Verbatim: "nearly half (44%) of foodservice companies reported feeling less confident in forecasting week-to-week, compared to just 12% that feel more confident". As stated: delaying maintenance 51%, delaying capital investment 49%, postponing expansion/renovation 48%; "nearly two-thirds of operators" in quick-service reported reduced employee hours.

**4 May 2026 — news release, "High operating costs and uneven consumer spending put Canada's restaurant sector, a key economic engine, under pressure"** (The Quarterly, Q1 2026), https://www.restaurantscanada.org/high-operating-costs-and-uneven-consumer-spending-put-canadas-restaurant-sector-a-key-economic-engine-under-pressure/ (opened 28 Sep 2026). As stated: 2025 nominal sales growth 2.3%; 2026 real-sales forecast −0.2%; 36% of operators at a loss or breaking even ("triple the 2019 level"); 71% report declining profitability; 49% report lower sales in early 2026; 54% report fewer guests; quick-service: 81% report declining profitability vs 70% in full-service; 91% cite food costs, 87% labour costs; 69% say customers are dining out less because of affordability. Higginson (verbatim): "The restaurant industry is typically the first to feel economic pressure when Canadians are struggling." Note: "54% report fewer guests" is the closest thing to a traffic statement in the 2026 releases; it is an operator-survey share, not a measured traffic index.

**Economist's Notebook index page** https://www.restaurantscanada.org/economists-notebook/ (opened 28 Sep 2026) shows a post titled "Restaurants Canada's CDI Continues to Underperform in March 2026" with the verbatim teaser: the Consumer Dining Index "came in at 85.9, down 6.4%". The post itself was not opened (URL not exposed on the index page); do not use the 85.9 figure without opening it.

**3 Nov 2025 — news release, "Foodservice sector added nearly 24,000 jobs since start of the year, but inflation and affordability continue to squeeze profit margins"** (The Quarterly, Q3 2025), https://www.restaurantscanada.org/foodservice-sector-added-nearly-24000-jobs-since-start-of-the-year-but-inflation-and-affordability-continue-to-squeeze-profit-margins/ (opened 28 Sep 2026). As stated: commercial foodservice revenue up 6.9% in the first seven months of 2025; 5.4% nominal growth expected for 2025; real growth 2.1% expected for 2025 and a 0.7% decline projected for 2026; two-year cost increases: insurance +14%, food +13%, labour +11%; 74% of Canadians cutting discretionary spending, 56% reducing eating out, 50% cutting take-out/delivery. Higginson (partial verbatim): "Operating costs continue to rise while consumers are pulling back on discretionary spending".

**22 Sep 2025 — news release, "Canadians are snacking more, drinking less and looking for more value for their shrinking dollar: 2025 Foodservice Facts report"**, https://www.restaurantscanada.org/canadians-are-snacking-more-drinking-less-and-looking-for-more-value-for-their-shrinking-dollar-2025-foodservice-facts-report/ (opened 28 Sep 2026). Verbatim: "Three in four Canadians (75%) are eating out less often due to the rising cost of living". As stated: 81% among ages 18–34; per-capita spending at quick-service $1,135 (down from $1,150 in 2019) and full-service $1,035 (down from $1,165 in 2019); quick-service lunchtime traffic up 7.6%, exceeding pre-pandemic levels. Higginson (verbatim): "While conditions have improved somewhat over the past year, this is still a very challenging market, as Canadians continue to face an affordability crisis and rising operational costs are squeezing operators' margins." Boilerplate: "$124 billion foodservice industry".

**4 Jun 2025 — news release, "Feds could add up to 80,000 new jobs, save Canadians $5.4B in tax by permanently removing GST from all food"**, https://www.restaurantscanada.org/feds-could-add-up-to-80000-new-jobs-save-canadians-5-4b-in-tax-by-permanently-removing-gst-from-all-food/ (opened 28 Sep 2026). Verbatim: "January and February 2025 also saw a 50% year-over-year decrease in foodservice bankruptcies" (no data source named in the release); the GST/HST holiday "led to a 8.6% increase in commercial foodservice sales in January".

**22 May 2025 — statement on WorkSafeBC gratuities policy**, https://www.restaurantscanada.org/worksafe-bcs-confusing-and-contradictory-gratuities-as-assessed-payroll-policy-must-be-immediately-paused-and-reassessed/ (opened 28 Sep 2026). Verbatim: "In 2024 restaurant bankruptcies hit record levels and more than half of BC's restaurants are unprofitable as they contend with large inflationary operating cost increases, declining consumer spending and labour shortages." No source cited in the statement. Do not present "record levels" as an independent finding; it is the association's assertion.

**1 Apr 2025 — news release, "Federal parties can protect jobs and affordability by permanently exempting all food from GST/HST in face of new U.S. tariffs"**, https://www.restaurantscanada.org/federal-parties-can-protect-jobs-and-affordability-by-permanently-exempting-all-food-from-gst-hst-in-face-of-new-u-s-tariffs/ (opened 28 Sep 2026). Verbatim: "53% of foodservice businesses say they are operating at a loss or just breaking even, up from 12% pre-pandemic." As stated: bankruptcies increased 45% in the first eight months of 2024 vs the same period of 2023 (source not named in the release); January 2025 commercial foodservice sales +7.5% YoY, real +4.3% (attributed to Statistics Canada); consumer survey by spark*insights, 13–19 Feb 2025.

**Industry-news index** https://www.restaurantscanada.org/industry-news/ (opened 28 Sep 2026) additionally shows, with month only: "Restaurant sales are expected to reach $130 billion in 2026" (Sep 2026); "We generate $125 billion in annual sales" (federal pre-budget submission, May 2026); "Restaurant sales are forecast to grow by 3.7% in the first quarter of 2025" (Quarterly, Jan 2025); "Restaurant transactions increased by 7.6 percentage points on average during the GST and HST holiday" (Feb 2025).

What Restaurants Canada does NOT publish openly (checked 28 Sep 2026): monthly "Fast Facts" (https://www.restaurantscanada.org/fast-facts/), "Foodservice Facts" full reports ($250 non-member, https://www.restaurantscanada.org/foodservice-facts/) and "The Quarterly" are members-only; no public same-store-sales series or closure count series exists on the site. Any "same-store sales" figure for the industry must therefore come from company filings, not from Restaurants Canada.

## 4 Competition Bureau, court and class-action record 2022–2026 touching Canadian quick-service chicken chains
Status labels per house rules. Nothing here is a finding of wrongdoing; every item below is an allegation or a regulatory process unless marked otherwise.

### 4.1 Court / class action records found (tier a — filed court documents hosted by the CBA National Class Action Database, https://cbaapps.org/ClassAction/Search.aspx, searched 28 Sep 2026)

**(i) Reddick c. Kentucky Fried Chicken Canada Company et al., Quebec Superior Court (Class Actions Chamber), District of Montréal, No. 500-06-001436-258.** ALLEGATION — authorization PENDING (CBA status "Not certified" as of 28 Sep 2026).
- Application for authorization to institute a class action, dated "Montréal, 7 novembre 2025", filed by Me Sébastien A. Paquette (Services Juridiques SP Inc.) for plaintiff Brandyn Reddick; 151 pages. Document: https://cbaapps.org/ClassAction/PDF.aspx?id=26996 (opened and read 28 Sep 2026). CBA record: received 13 Nov 2025, posted 21 Nov 2025, subjects "Consumer Protection / Competition".
- Defendants (from the application and the Moneris response): Kentucky Fried Chicken Canada Company (191 Creditview Road, Suite 100, Vaughan ON), Five Guys Enterprises LLC, Koeppel Companies ULC, Restaurant Que-Bouffe Inc., several numbered Quebec/Canada companies, Beiko La Boîte à Beignes Inc., Dévamc Canada Inc., Kinton Kinka Canada Inc., Groupe Sportscene Inc., Groupe Madame Poulet Inc., Mandy's Salads Inc., Mr. Puffs Canada Inc., Ange Canada Inc., Groupe No. 990 Pizzéria Napolitaine Inc., Restaurants Pacini Inc., Gestion Familiale Giguère Inc., Foodtastic Inc., Foodtastic Franchises Inc., Milestones Restaurants Ltd., Souvlaki Bar Franchises Inc., Franchises MTY Inc., Groupe d'alimentation MTY Inc., Franchises Cora Inc., S.I.N.I. Groupe Inc., Recipe Unlimited Corporation, Corporation Solutions Moneris. (Of these, KFC and Groupe Madame Poulet are the chicken-focused brands; the action is an industry-wide "junk fee" case, not KFC-specific.)
- What is alleged (verbatim, French, from para. 1 of the application): "Un premier prix plus bas mais inatteignable annoncé aux consommateurs. Des frais obligatoires fixes qui n'apportent pourtant aucune valeur additionnelle, systématiquement ajoutés plus tard. La présente action collective vise à mettre fin à cette pratique d'indication de prix partiel et de décomposition de prix pratiquée au Québec par plusieurs grandes chaînes de restauration, dont Poulet Frit Kentucky (« PFK »), pour les commandes de repas livrés effectuées via leurs applications mobiles ou sites Internet, obtenir un remède pour les membres du groupe ainsi que des dommages punitifs". Legal bases pleaded: Quebec Consumer Protection Act ss. 219, 224(c), 228; Competition Act ss. 52, 52(1.1), 52(1.3) and s. 36 recovery. Example pleaded (para. 9): a "Fameux sandwich PFK à la carte" shown at $11.29 on the PFK app before delivery fees are added at checkout.
- Proposed class (verbatim): "Tous les consommateurs au sens de la Loi sur la protection du consommateur au Québec qui, depuis le 7 novembre 2022, ont effectué une commande de repas livré via le site internet ou les applications mobiles de l'une des Défenderesses et ont payé un prix supérieur (en raison de frais ajoutés obligatoires) au prix initialement annoncé ou indiqué pour pouvoir compléter leur commande." Punitive damages sought: $1,000,000 to $5,000,000 per defendant depending on size.
- Procedural record: Order of designation, 16 March 2026, Sheehan J.C.S., designating Justice Eleni Yiannakis to hear the authorization application — https://cbaapps.org/ClassAction/PDF.aspx?id=27731 (opened 28 Sep 2026). Response of defendant Corporation Solutions Moneris (Blake, Cassels & Graydon), 19 Nov 2025, stating it intends to contest authorization — https://cbaapps.org/ClassAction/PDF.aspx?id=27098 (opened 28 Sep 2026). Fifteen further defendant responses are listed (not opened).
- KFC's position (COMPANY STATEMENT in a court filing, not a finding): "Application by Defendant Kentucky Fried Chicken Canada Company for Leave to Adduce Relevant Evidence", Osler, Hoskin & Harcourt LLP, dated "MONTRÉAL, July 13, 2026", with the sworn statement of Ryan Fisher, Martech Product Manager — https://cbaapps.org/ClassAction/PDF.aspx?id=28376 (opened 28 Sep 2026). Verbatim from the application: "The Application contains certain vague and imprecise allegations. Therefore, KFC seeks leave from this Court to adduce relevant evidence in order to rectify incorrect allegations and to correct facts that are necessary for the purposes of the authorization hearing." Verbatim from the Fisher sworn statement: "Kentucky Fried Chicken Canada Company is the franchisor of the KFC system ... KFC restaurants in Canada, which are owned and operated by independent franchisees." ; "Each franchisee is responsible for determining the delivery fee applicable to orders that it delivers, notably by working directly with a Delivery as a Service (DaaS) provider, such as Uber Eats." ; "Neither Kentucky Fried Chicken Canada Company nor Cognizant is involved in setting or determining the delivery fees applicable to orders placed online by customers for delivery." ; "On or around January 23, 2026, Kentucky Fried Chicken Canada Company began implementing changes to update and improve its online ordering platforms, including the www.kfc.ca website and the mobile application." ; "As of March 18, 2026, the updated website became completely operational." ; "as of March 25, 2026, the updated mobile application became completely operational." ; the updated platforms show that "The delivery fee is visible prior to selecting the restaurant" and "the delivery fee is visible throughout the ordering journey on the information banner."
- How to use: this is a documented, dated 2025–2026 court record touching KFC Canada (an allegation, pending). The Jan–Mar 2026 platform change is the company's own sworn description of a change to how delivery fees are displayed; the filing does not say why the change was made, so do not describe it as an admission or as a response to the lawsuit.

**(ii) Keswani and Sivertson v. PH Canada Company et al. (incl. Popeyes Louisiana Kitchen, Inc., Restaurant Brands International Inc., and Vancouver Fried Chicken Corp. dba BB.Q Chicken Canada), Supreme Court of British Columbia, Vancouver Registry, Court File No. VLC-S-S-254553.** ALLEGATION — not certified (CBA status "Not Certified" as of 28 Sep 2026).

## 5 Franchise law context — Ontario Arthur Wishart Act (Franchise Disclosure), 2000, S.O. 2000, c. 3
Tier (a). Statute URL (official, e-Laws; JavaScript required): https://www.ontario.ca/laws/statute/00a03 . Text read today from the Internet Archive capture of that e-Laws page (captured 24 Jan 2025; page header: "current consolidated laws on e-Laws are current (up-to-date) to November 1, 2024"): https://web.archive.org/web/20250124062637id_/https://www.ontario.ca/laws/statute/00a03 . Regulation: O. Reg. 581/00 (General), https://www.ontario.ca/laws/regulation/000581 (archived copy read: https://web.archive.org/web/20241213014521id_/https://www.ontario.ca/laws/regulation/000581 ; consolidation period "From September 1, 2020"; last amendment 418/20). Both opened 28 Sep 2026.

One paragraph for the script: Ontario's Arthur Wishart Act (Franchise Disclosure), 2000 requires a franchisor to give a prospective franchisee a single disclosure document "not less than 14 days before the earlier of" the signing of the franchise agreement (or any other agreement relating to the franchise, other than a confidentiality/site-designation agreement) and "the payment of any consideration" (s. 5(1)); the document "shall contain, (a) all material facts, including material facts as prescribed; (b) financial statements as prescribed; (c) copies of all proposed franchise agreements and other agreements relating to the franchise ... (d) statements as prescribed for the purposes of assisting the prospective franchisee in making informed investment decisions; and (e) other information and copies of documents as prescribed" (s. 5(4)), and "All information in a disclosure document and a statement of a material change shall be accurately, clearly and concisely set out" (s. 5(6)), with a written statement of any material change owed "as soon as practicable after the change has occurred" (s. 5(5)). Every franchise agreement "imposes on each party a duty of fair dealing in its performance and enforcement", which "includes the duty to act in good faith and in accordance with reasonable commercial standards" (s. 3), and franchisees "may associate with other franchisees and may form or join an organization of franchisees", which the franchisor "shall not interfere with, prohibit or restrict" or penalize (s. 4). The remedies are the reason franchisee lawsuits cluster around disclosure: a franchisee "may rescind the franchise agreement, without penalty or obligation, no later than 60 days after receiving the disclosure document" if it was late or deficient (s. 6(1)), or "no later than two years after entering into the franchise agreement if the franchisor never provided the disclosure document" (s. 6(2)), after which the franchisor must within 60 days refund money paid, buy back inventory, supplies and equipment at cost and "compensate the franchisee for any losses that the franchisee incurred in acquiring, setting up and operating the franchise" (s. 6(6)); separately, a franchisee who "suffers a loss because of a misrepresentation contained in the disclosure document ... or as a result of the franchisor's failure to comply in any way with section 5" has a right of action for damages against the franchisor, its agent, broker and associate and "every person who signed the disclosure document" (s. 7(1)), with reliance on a misrepresentation "deemed" (s. 7(2)). Exemptions include grants to existing franchisees of a substantially similar additional franchise, renewals with no material change, and franchises below or above prescribed total-investment thresholds (s. 5(7)). Note for the team: the Act is Ontario-only; Alberta, BC, Manitoba, New Brunswick and PEI have their own franchise statutes and Quebec has none — a chain's FDD obligations differ by province.

## 6 Chicken input costs 2023–2026 (tier a)

### 6.1 Chicken Farmers of Canada, "2026 Chicken Data Booklet / Livret des données sur le poulet" (PDF, downloaded and text-extracted 28 Sep 2026)
URL: https://www.chickenfarmers.ca/wp-content/uploads/2026/08/2026-Data-Booklet-Livret-des-donnees-sur-le-poulet.pdf (linked from https://www.chickenfarmers.ca/resources/ , opened 28 Sep 2026). CFC states it represents "2,921 farmers"; "In 2025, Canadians each consumed 35.6 kilograms of chicken."

**Average Producer Prices for Broilers (¢/kg, live weight)** — "SOURCE: CFC, using provincial board information" (booklet p. 17). Columns: B.C. | Alta. | Sask. | Man. | Ont. | Que. | N.B. | N.S. | Weighted average.
- 2018: 162.4 | 159.0 | 158.2 | 158.5 | 151.5 | 152.3 | 162.5 | 161.4 | **155.3**
- 2019: 166.6 | 162.7 | 161.0 | 162.0 | 155.0 | 155.8 | 164.2 | 164.6 | **158.8**
- 2020: 169.7 | 165.6 | 163.9 | 163.9 | 157.5 | 158.5 | 166.8 | 169.0 | **161.5**
- 2021: 191.4 | 186.7 | 188.2 | 187.6 | 179.1 | 179.9 | 188.2 | 189.1 | **183.1**
- 2022: 214.6 | 211.0 | 213.2 | 212.3 | 199.1 | 199.9 | 208.3 | 208.0 | **204.3**
- 2023: 225.1 | 221.9 | 224.6 | 218.5 | 206.1 | 207.7 | 216.1 | 219.2 | **212.6**
- 2024: 222.0 | 216.9 | 214.7 | 213.0 | 200.7 | 202.5 | 210.9 | 219.7 | **207.7**
- 2025: 225.7 | 211.1 | 207.5 | 207.7 | 201.4 | 203.6 | 211.9 | 222.1 | **207.8**
Computed: the national weighted-average live price rose 31.6% from 2019 (158.8) to 2023 (212.6), then eased 2.3% to 207.7 in 2024 and was flat in 2025 (207.8). Ontario live price 2025 (201.4) is 2.3% below its 2023 level (206.1). Script use: chicken farm-gate cost pressure peaked in 2022–23 and has been flat-to-lower since — a chain citing "rising chicken costs" for 2024–26 menu increases is not supported by the farm-gate series (processor/wholesale is a separate step — see below).

**Wholesale Price Series (¢/kg, eviscerated weight)** — "SOURCE: EMI (Express Markets, Inc.) – a commodities reporting service"; "Based on invoice data provided by ten (as at December 31, 2024) Canadian processors covering a significant portion of the total Canadian wholesale volume" (booklet p. 18). Annual averages, 2016 → 2025:
- Market composite: 362, 382, 364, 360, 355, 391, 443, 435, 433, **500**
- Whole bird complex: 364, 376, 375, 374, 373, 400, 463, 471, 474, **529**
- Breast complex: 551, 605, 570, 548, 518, 577, 660, 595, 580, **721**
- Wing complex: 556, 555, 503, 490, 517, 623, 629, 574, 528, **601**
- Leg complex: 262, 274, 246, 249, 252, 277, 306, 332, 344, **389**
Computed: wholesale market composite 2025 vs 2024 +15.5%; breast complex +24.3%; wing complex +13.8%; whole bird +11.6%. So the 2025 cost story for a fried-chicken operator is at the processor/wholesale step, not the farm gate. (Monthly 2026 wholesale values were not extracted; CFC posts monthly "Market Updates" at https://www.chickenfarmers.ca/sections/market-update/ — not opened today.)

**Consumer Price Index for Chicken and Other Items (2002=100)** — booklet p. 21, "SOURCE: Statistics Canada, Table 18-10-0005-01": Chicken 2019 164.5; 2020 169.8; 2021 179.2; 2022 193.0; 2023 210.7; 2024 216.2; 2025 221.3 (computed: 2025 vs 2024 +2.4%; 2022→2025 +14.7%). Same table, Food: 2022 171.5; 2023 184.3; 2024 189.3; 2025 195.3. All-items: 2022 151.2; 2023 157.1; 2024 160.9; 2025 164.2.

**Retail prices**: booklet pp. 19–20 carry "Average Retail Prices for Broilers ($/kg, eviscerated weight)" and "Average Retail Prices for Selected Parts ($/kg, eviscerated weight)" 2023–2025 by province, "SOURCE: The Nielsen Company, compiled by Agriculture and Agri-Food Canada, Poultry Section" (AAFC Report 116). The extracted text loses the column headers for the parts table; values are therefore NOT reproduced here (see section 9).

CAUTION — do not use: the booklet's pp. 25–26 table "FARM PRICE (LIVE WEIGHT) / WHOLESALE – READY TO COOK (RTC) / AVERAGE RETAIL PRICE" (2025: 71.6 / 124.8 / 206.0) is the **U.S.** series in U.S. ¢/lb (source USDA / U.S. BLS), not Canadian data.

### 6.2 Agriculture and Agri-Food Canada — Poultry and egg market information
- "Chicken" sector page, https://agriculture.canada.ca/en/sector/animal-industry/poultry-and-egg-market-information/chicken (date modified 2026-06-24; opened 28 Sep 2026). As stated: 2025 production 1.4 billion kg eviscerated; Ontario and Quebec 61.0% of output; 2,834 regulated producers; per-capita consumption 35.64 kg in 2025, "slightly higher than the 2024 level"; "Chicken remained the most consumed animal meat protein in Canada in 2025"; imports 231.4 million kg ($1.1 billion); exports 106.7 million kg ($754 million); farm cash receipts $4 billion, 4% of agricultural cash receipts; over 70 primary processing establishments (36 federally inspected).
- "Prices" page, https://agriculture.canada.ca/en/sector/animal-industry/poultry-and-egg-market-information/prices (date modified 2025-11-24; opened 28 Sep 2026), links to the AIMIS report generator: Report 032 "Chicken producer prices" (weekly low/high/average by province; week-end dates available through 2026-09-19 as of today), https://agriculture.canada.ca/en/market-information-system/rp/index-eng.cfm?action=pR&r=23&pdctc= ; Report 041 "Monthly and annual average poultry producer prices" (2008–2026), …&r=20&pdctc= ; Report 030 "Annual most likely prices to chicken producers by allocation period", …&r=21&pdctc= ; Report 116 retail poultry prices, …&r=116&pdctc= (all report pages opened 28 Sep 2026; date modified 2026-09-17). The generator requires an arithmetic CAPTCHA and did not return report output to this session's scripted requests, so no AIMIS values are quoted here; the CFC booklet series above is CFC's compilation of the same provincial-board producer prices.

## 7 Consumer and media record (tier d)
Only named surveys with publisher, year and stated methodology qualify. From today's sources:
- Restaurants Canada, Foodservice Facts 2026 consumer survey (released 24 Sep 2026): "Eight in 10 Canadians (80%) are eating out less often due to the rising cost of living, up from 75%". Methodology paragraph was not captured by the fetch — obtain sample size/dates from the release before use.
- Restaurants Canada, Foodservice Facts 2025 (22 Sep 2025): "Three in four Canadians (75%) are eating out less often due to the rising cost of living".
- Restaurants Canada / spark*insights consumer survey, 13–19 Feb 2025 (cited in the 1 Apr 2025 release): 77% want the GST/HST holiday made permanent; 84% believe food should not be taxed.
- Restaurants Canada operator surveys (Restaurant Outlook Survey, in The Quarterly): 36% at a loss or break-even (Q1 2026, released 4 May 2026); 41% (Q2 2026, released 20 Jul 2026); 53% (release of 1 Apr 2025); "12%" in 2019/pre-pandemic. These are association surveys of members/operators; no brand-level or chicken-segment cut is public.
No brand-trust ranking meeting the house standard was opened today. Review-site material: none used.

## 8 VERDICT for the script
**NONE** — this is the industry-context file; it establishes no chain-specific "price paid". Context findings the chain dossiers can lean on (all tier a):
1. Menu inflation has cooled: StatCan CPI "food purchased from restaurants" +3.1% YoY in Aug 2026 (fast food & take-out +2.3%), down from +5.9% for fast food in Aug 2025; grocery inflation (+2.8%) now runs below all-items (+3.0%). Two-year change Aug 2024→Aug 2026: fast food +8.3% vs grocery +6.3%.
2. Dollar sales are still growing (+6.0% YoY June 2026, seasonally adjusted; limited-service +6.1%), but Restaurants Canada's own releases say 41% of operators are at a loss or break-even (Jul 2026) and 54% report fewer guests (May 2026); the only public "traffic" measures are survey shares, not counts.
3. Input costs: farm-gate live chicken price flat since 2023 (207.7–207.8 ¢/kg in 2024–25 vs 212.6 in 2023), while the processor/wholesale composite jumped 15.5% in 2025 (breast +24.3%) — attribute any 2025–26 "chicken cost" claim to the wholesale step, with the EMI/CFC source.
4. Live legal theme: two pending drip-pricing class actions name chicken brands among many defendants — Reddick c. KFC Canada et al. (QC, filed 7 Nov 2025, authorization pending) and Keswani v. PH Canada et al. incl. Popeyes/RBI/BB.Q Chicken (BC, filed 18 Jun 2025, not certified). Allegations only.

- Restaurants Canada "record" bankruptcies in 2024 (22 May 2025 statement) and "45%" increase in bankruptcies Jan–Aug 2024 (1 Apr 2025 release): no data source named by the association; the underlying OSB insolvency statistics were NOT opened today. Do not air as fact; if needed, pull Office of the Superintendent of Bankruptcy NAICS 722 insolvency tables directly.
- Restaurants Canada Consumer Dining Index "85.9, down 6.4%" (March 2026): teaser text only; post not opened; methodology unknown.
- Restaurants Canada 2026 pre-tax margin "4.1%" and "24 million visits daily": from the Foodservice Facts 2026 release as summarized; exact sentence not captured verbatim — re-open before quoting.
- Any 2024 Restaurants Canada release (e.g., Foodservice Facts 2024 launch, 2024 Quarterly figures): not opened today; the association's 2024 statements are not in this file except as later releases restate them.
- CFC booklet retail price tables for broilers and parts ($/kg, Nielsen via AAFC): values not reproduced because column headers were lost in text extraction; open the PDF pp. 19–20 visually before use.
- AAFC AIMIS weekly/monthly producer price values 2023–2026 (Reports 032/041/030): pages opened, report output not retrieved (CAPTCHA); use CFC's compilation (section 6.1) or run the report manually.
- CFC monthly "Market Update" 2026 wholesale figures: not opened.
- Competition Bureau releases dated 1 Jan 2022 – 27 Nov 2024: outside the 100-entry feed window pulled today; not swept.
- Status after filing of the DoorDash Tribunal application (9 Jun 2025), of Keswani v. PH Canada et al. (BC, 18 Jun 2025), and any authorization ruling in Reddick c. KFC (QC): not verified today because the Tribunal, BC Courts and CanLII were unreachable. Treat all three as pending.
- StatCan Table 33-10-0270-01 "closing businesses" measure for NAICS 72: not pulled (only active/opening/continuing were). The table is experimental and covers accommodation and food services together; it cannot be used for restaurants alone or for any chain.
- Ontario e-Laws consolidation later than 1 Nov 2024: the live page could not be rendered; check for amendments to the Wishart Act after that date before airing statutory wording.
- Anything recalled from memory about specific franchisee suits against Mary Brown's, Popeyes Canada, KFC Canada (Priszm-era), Chick-fil-A Canada or Church's Texas Chicken Canada: NOT searched successfully today (CanLII blocked; CBA database is class actions only) — belongs in the chain dossiers, not here.
